--- Strategy Report Received ---
Here is the scientific strategy for solving the KdV equation using a Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN).

### 1. Scientific Strategy

**Subject:** Forward Solution of the Korteweg-De Vries (KdV) Equation (Two-Soliton Interaction).

**Goal:** Train a PINN to approximate $u(x,t)$ consistent with the KdV equation and the provided initial/boundary conditions derived from the analytical solution.

**Key Challenges:**
1.  **High-Order Derivatives:** The term $u_{xxx}$ makes the loss landscape rough and sensitive to noise.
2.  **Non-Linearity:** The convection term $6uu_x$ introduces strong non-linearity.
3.  **Sharp Gradients:** Soliton interactions create localized, high-frequency features that standard networks often smooth out (spectral bias).

### 2. Detailed Plan

#### 2.1. Data Source & Generation
Since this is a forward problem with a known analytical solution used for BCs, we will generate synthetic training data.

*   **Analytical Solution Implementation:**
    *   Implement the function `u_analytical(t, x)` using JAX.
    *   **Parameters:** $c_1 = 6.0, c_2 = 2.0, x_1 = -2.0, x_2 = 2.0$.
    *   **Variables:**
        *   $\zeta_1 = x - c_1 t - x_1$
        *   $\zeta_2 = x - c_2 t - x_2$
    *   **Formula:** Implement the provided fraction exactly.
    *   **Neumann Data Generation:** To generate the ground truth for the Neumann BC ($g_3(t)$), do **not** derive the formula manually. Use JAX's automatic differentiation (`jax.grad` w.r.t `x`) on the `u_analytical` function to evaluate $u_x(t, 20)$.

*   **Training Data Sampling:**
    *   **Domain:** $t \in [0, 5]$, $x \in [0, 20]$.
    *   **Collocation Points ($N_f$):** Sample 10,000 points inside the domain using Latin Hypercube Sampling (LHS) or Uniform Random Sampling. **Constraint:** Do NOT use `criterion='maximin'`.
    *   **Boundary/Initial Points ($N_b$):**
        *   Initial ($t=0$): 200 points.
        *   Left Boundary ($x=0$): 200 points.
        *   Right Boundary ($x=20$): 200 points.
    *   **Validation Data:** Create a high-resolution grid (e.g., $256 \times 100$) to compute the relative $L_2$ error against the analytical solution.

#### 2.2. Problem Statement

**Governing Equation (Residual):**
The PDE residual $f(t,x)$ to be minimized is:
$$ f = u_t + 6u u_x + u_{xxx} $$

**Boundary & Initial Conditions:**
1.  **Initial Condition (Dirichlet):**
    $$ u(0, x) = u_{analytical}(0, x) $$
2.  **Left Boundary (Dirichlet):**
    $$ u(t, 0) = u_{analytical}(t, 0) $$
3.  **Right Boundary (Dirichlet):**
    $$ u(t, 20) = u_{analytical}(t, 20) $$
4.  **Right Boundary (Neumann):**
    $$ u_x(t, 20) = \frac{\partial u_{analytical}}{\partial x}(t, 20) $$

#### 2.3. Model Architecture
*   **Network:** Fully Connected Neural Network (MLP).
*   **Depth/Width:** 5 hidden layers with 64 neurons each.
*   **Activation:** `tanh` (Hyperbolic Tangent). This is crucial for calculating smooth higher-order derivatives ($u_{xxx}$).
*   **Inputs:** $(t, x)$ normalized if necessary (though domain $[0,5] \times [0,20]$ is manageable, normalization to $[-1, 1]$ is recommended for stability).
*   **Output:** $u(t, x)$.

#### 2.4. Optimization Strategy
*   **Optimizer:** `optax.adam` (JAX native). **NO SciPy/L-BFGS-B**.
*   **Learning Rate:** Exponential Decay schedule.
    *   Start: $1e-3$
    *   Decay rate: $0.9$ every 2000 steps.
    *   End: $\approx 1e-5$.
*   **Iterations:** 40,000 steps. (KdV requires long training times to resolve the third derivative).
*   **Reporting:** Print Loss, PDE component, BC component, and Relative L2 Error every **500** iterations.

#### 2.5. Loss Function
$$ \mathcal{L}_{total} = \lambda_{PDE} \mathcal{L}_{PDE} + \lambda_{IC} \mathcal{L}_{IC} + \lambda_{BC} \mathcal{L}_{BC} + \lambda_{Neumann} \mathcal{L}_{Neumann} $$

Where $\mathcal{L}$ is the Mean Squared Error (MSE).

**Weighting:**
*   $\lambda_{PDE} = 1.0$
*   $\lambda_{IC} = 10.0$ (Hard constraint on initial state is vital for time evolution).
*   $\lambda_{BC} = 10.0$
*   $\lambda_{Neumann} = 1.0$

### 3. Output Section

**Main Model:** MLP (5 layers, 64 units, Tanh) optimized with Adam (Optax) for 40,000 iterations.
**Enhancements:**
1.  **JAX AD for Ground Truth:** Use `jax.grad` on the analytical function to generate exact Neumann boundary data, avoiding manual derivation errors.
2.  **Input Normalization:** Scale inputs $(t, x)$ to range $[-1, 1]$ before passing to the network to improve spectral convergence.

--- Analizing: # ## Cell 3: Data Generation ---
  - [Planner]: Instructions received:
### [CURRENT_CELL_ID]
* **Action:** Replace the placeholder content with the data generation logic. This involves implementing the analytical two-soliton solution for the specified KdV equation, using JAX's automatic differentiation to derive the Neumann boundary data, sampling points for training using LHS and uniform sampling, and creating a high-resolution grid for validation.
* **New Code:**
    ```python
    # --- Analytical and Ground Truth Data Generation ---

    @jit
    def u_analytical_scalar(t, x):
        """
        Analytical two-soliton solution for the KdV equation form: u_t + 6uu_x + u_xxx = 0.
        This is the negative of the standard solution for the more common form u_t - 6uu_x + u_xxx = 0.
        """
        c1, c2, x1, x2 = args.c1, args.c2, args.x1, args.x2
        k1, k2 = jnp.sqrt(c1), jnp.sqrt(c2)
        A = ((k1 - k2) / (k1 + k2))**2

        # Define a function of x only (for a given t) to compute d^2/dx^2
        def log_tau_func(x_):
            eta1 = k1 * (x_ - c1 * t - x1)
            eta2 = k2 * (x_ - c2 * t - x2)
            tau = 1 + jnp.exp(eta1) + jnp.exp(eta2) + A * jnp.exp(eta1 + eta2)
            return jnp.log(tau)

        # u(x,t) = -2 * d^2/dx^2 [log(tau)]
        d2_log_tau_dx2 = jax.grad(jax.grad(log_tau_func))(x)
        return -2.0 * d2_log_tau_dx2

    # Vectorize the scalar function to handle entire arrays of (t, x) points
    u_analytical = jit(vmap(u_analytical_scalar, in_axes=(0, 0), out_axes=0))

    # Create the Neumann boundary condition ground truth function u_x(t, x) via automatic differentiation
    @jit
    def u_x_analytical_scalar(t, x):
        return jax.grad(u_analytical_scalar, argnums=1)(t, x)
    u_x_analytical = jit(vmap(u_x_analytical_scalar, in_axes=(0, 0), out_axes=0))


    # --- Training Data Sampling ---

    # Domain boundaries
    t_min, t_max = args.t_domain
    x_min, x_max = args.x_domain

    # Collocation points (for PDE residual) using Latin Hypercube Sampling
    lhs_samples = lhs(2, samples=args.N_f)
    t_f = t_min + (t_max - t_min) * lhs_samples[:, 0:1]
    x_f = x_min + (x_max - x_min) * lhs_samples[:, 1:2]

    # Create a new random key for sampling to ensure reproducibility
    key, subkey = jax.random.split(args.key)

    # Initial condition points (t=0)
    x_ic = jax.random.uniform(subkey, (args.N_b, 1), minval=x_min, maxval=x_max)
    t_ic = jnp.full_like(x_ic, t_min)
    u_ic = u_analytical(t_ic.flatten(), x_ic.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)

    # Left boundary points (x=0)
    key, subkey = jax.random.split(key)
    t_bc0 = jax.random.uniform(subkey, (args.N_b, 1), minval=t_min, maxval=t_max)
    x_bc0 = jnp.full_like(t_bc0, x_min)
    u_bc0 = u_analytical(t_bc0.flatten(), x_bc0.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)

    # Right boundary points (x=20) for both Dirichlet and Neumann
    key, subkey = jax.random.split(key)
    t_bc1 = jax.random.uniform(subkey, (args.N_b, 1), minval=t_min, maxval=t_max)
    x_bc1 = jnp.full_like(t_bc1, x_max)
    u_bc1 = u_analytical(t_bc1.flatten(), x_bc1.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
    u_x_bc1 = u_x_analytical(t_bc1.flatten(), x_bc1.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)

    # --- Validation Data ---
    # Create a high-resolution grid for validation and plotting
    t_star = jnp.linspace(t_min, t_max, 256)
    x_star = jnp.linspace(x_min, x_max, 100)
    T, X = jnp.meshgrid(t_star, x_star, indexing='ij')
    t_val = T.flatten()
    x_val = X.flatten()
    Exact = u_analytical(t_val, x_val).reshape(T.shape)

    print(f"Generated {args.N_f} collocation points.")
    print(f"Generated {args.N_b} initial condition points (t=0).")
    print(f"Generated {args.N_b} boundary condition points (x=0 and x=20).")
    print(f"Validation grid shape: {Exact.shape}")
    ```

--- Analizing: # ## Cell 4: Model ---

### [CURRENT_CELL_ID]
* **Action:** Replace the placeholder with the Flax MLP model definition. The model will incorporate a differentiable input normalization layer to scale `t` and `x` to the `[-1, 1]` range, as specified by the scientific strategy. It will then be initialized with the configured architecture (5 hidden layers, 64 neurons, tanh activation).
* **New Code:**
    ```python
    class MLP(nn.Module):
        features: Sequence[int]
        activation: Callable = nn.tanh
        
        @nn.compact
        def __call__(self, t, x):
            # --- Input Normalization Layer ---
            # Scale t from [t_min, t_max] to [-1, 1]
            t_min, t_max = args.t_domain
            t_norm = 2.0 * (t - t_min) / (t_max - t_min) - 1.0
            
            # Scale x from [x_min, x_max] to [-1, 1]
            x_min, x_max = args.x_domain
            x_norm = 2.0 * (x - x_min) / (x_max - x_min) - 1.0
            
            # Concatenate normalized inputs
            X = jnp.hstack([t_norm, x_norm])
            
            # --- MLP Layers ---
            for i, units in enumerate(self.features[:-1]):
                X = nn.Dense(features=units)(X)
                X = self.activation(X)
            
            # Final layer (no activation)
            X = nn.Dense(features=self.features[-1])(X)
            return X

    # Instantiate the model
    model = MLP(features=args.layers, activation=args.activation)

    # Initialize parameters
    # Create a dummy input with the correct shape (N, 1) for t and x
    dummy_t = jnp.ones((1, 1))
    dummy_x = jnp.ones((1, 1))
    params = model.init(args.key, dummy_t, dummy_x)['params']

    # Define the model's apply function for predictions
    apply_fn = jit(model.apply)

    print(f"Model initialized with {sum(p.size for p in jax.tree_util.tree_leaves(params))} parameters.")
    ```

--- Analizing: # ## Cell 5: Optimizer Setup ---

### [CURRENT_CELL_ID]
* **Action:** Replace the placeholder content with the core optimization components. This includes defining the KdV PDE residual function using JAX's automatic differentiation, constructing the composite loss function with the specified weights, setting up the `optax.adam` optimizer with an exponential decay learning rate schedule, and creating the JIT-compiled `train_step` function.
* **New Code:**
    ```python
    # --- PDE Residual Function ---

    def PDE_residuals(params, t, x, apply_fn):
        """
        Computes the residual of the KdV equation: f = u_t + 6*u*u_x + u_xxx
        """
        # A scalar wrapper for the model's prediction function to enable automatic differentiation
        def u_scalar(t_s, x_s):
            # The model expects inputs of shape (N, 1), so we reshape the scalar inputs
            return apply_fn({'params': params}, t_s.reshape(1, -1), x_s.reshape(1, -1))[0, 0]

        # Create functions for derivatives using jax.grad
        u_t_fn = jax.grad(u_scalar, argnums=0)
        u_x_fn = jax.grad(u_scalar, argnums=1)
        u_xx_fn = jax.grad(u_x_fn, argnums=1)  # Differentiating u_x w.r.t x gives u_xx
        u_xxx_fn = jax.grad(u_xx_fn, argnums=1) # Differentiating u_xx w.r.t x gives u_xxx

        # Vectorize the derivative functions to compute them over batches of points
        u_t_vmap = vmap(u_t_fn)
        u_x_vmap = vmap(u_x_fn)
        u_xxx_vmap = vmap(u_xxx_fn)

        # Evaluate the model and its derivatives at the given points
        u_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t, x)
        u_t = u_t_vmap(t.flatten(), x.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
        u_x = u_x_vmap(t.flatten(), x.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
        u_xxx = u_xxx_vmap(t.flatten(), x.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)

        # Compute the PDE residual
        residual = u_t + 6.0 * u_pred * u_x + u_xxx
        return residual

    # --- Loss Function ---

    @jit
    def loss_fn(params):
        # 1. PDE Loss (Collocation points)
        f_pred = PDE_residuals(params, t_f, x_f, apply_fn)
        loss_pde = jnp.mean(f_pred**2)

        # 2. Initial Condition Loss (t=0)
        u_ic_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_ic, x_ic)
        loss_ic = jnp.mean((u_ic_pred - u_ic)**2)

        # 3. Boundary Condition Loss (Dirichlet at x=0 and x=20)
        u_bc0_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_bc0, x_bc0)
        u_bc1_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_bc1, x_bc1)
        loss_bc = jnp.mean((u_bc0_pred - u_bc0)**2) + jnp.mean((u_bc1_pred - u_bc1)**2)

        # 4. Neumann Boundary Condition Loss (u_x at x=20)
        def u_scalar_for_grad(t_s, x_s):
            return apply_fn({'params': params}, t_s.reshape(1,-1), x_s.reshape(1,-1))[0, 0]
        
        u_x_model_fn = vmap(jax.grad(u_scalar_for_grad, argnums=1))
        u_x_bc1_pred = u_x_model_fn(t_bc1.flatten(), x_bc1.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
        loss_neumann = jnp.mean((u_x_bc1_pred - u_x_bc1)**2)

        # Total weighted loss
        total_loss = (args.lambda_pde * loss_pde +
                      args.lambda_ic * loss_ic +
                      args.lambda_bc * loss_bc +
                      args.lambda_neumann * loss_neumann)
        
        loss_components = {
            "total": total_loss, "pde": loss_pde, "ic": loss_ic,
            "bc": loss_bc, "neumann": loss_neumann
        }
        return total_loss, loss_components

    # --- Optimizer Setup ---

    # Exponential decay learning rate schedule
    lr_schedule = optax.exponential_decay(
        init_value=args.learning_rate_start,
        transition_steps=args.decay_steps,
        decay_rate=args.decay_rate,
        staircase=False
    )

    # Adam optimizer
    optimizer = optax.adam(learning_rate=lr_schedule)
    opt_state = optimizer.init(params)

    # --- JIT-Compiled Training Step ---

    @jit
    def train_step(params, opt_state):
        # Use jax.value_and_grad to get loss and gradients
        (loss_val, loss_components), grads = value_and_grad(loss_fn, has_aux=True)(params)
        
        # Update optimizer state and parameters
        updates, opt_state = optimizer.update(grads, opt_state, params)
        params = optax.apply_updates(params, updates)
        
        return params, opt_state, loss_val, loss_components

    print("Optimizer and training step initialized.")
    ```

--- Analizing: # ## Cell 6: Training ---
  - [Planner]: Instructions received:
### [CURRENT_CELL_ID]
* **Action:** Replace the empty list initializations with the main training loop. This loop will run for the specified number of iterations, call the `train_step` function, and perform periodic logging and error calculation as required by the scientific strategy.
* **New Code:**
    ```python
    # --- Main Training Loop ---
    print("Starting training...")
    start_time = time.time()

    # Lists to store metrics
    all_its = []
    all_loss = []
    all_errors = []
    all_L_2 = []
    all_L_infty = []

    # Use tqdm for a progress bar
    pbar = tqdm(range(args.iterations), desc="Training PINN")

    for it in pbar:
        # Perform one training step
        params, opt_state, loss_val, loss_components = train_step(params, opt_state)

        # --- Reporting and Error Calculation ---
        if it % args.report_every == 0:
            # Predict solution on the validation grid
            u_pred_val = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_val.reshape(-1, 1), x_val.reshape(-1, 1))
            u_pred_val = u_pred_val.reshape(Exact.shape)

            # Calculate relative L2 error
            error_l2 = jnp.linalg.norm(u_pred_val - Exact) / jnp.linalg.norm(Exact)
            
            # Calculate L-infinity error
            error_linf = jnp.max(jnp.abs(u_pred_val - Exact))

            # Store metrics
            all_its.append(it)
            all_loss.append(loss_val.item())
            all_errors.append(error_l2.item())
            all_L_2.append(jnp.linalg.norm(u_pred_val - Exact).item())
            all_L_infty.append(error_linf.item())

            # Update progress bar description
            pbar.set_postfix({
                "Loss": f"{loss_val.item():.4e}",
                "L2 Error": f"{error_l2.item():.4e}",
                "PDE": f"{loss_components['pde']:.2e}",
                "IC": f"{loss_components['ic']:.2e}",
                "BC": f"{loss_components['bc']:.2e}",
                "Neumann": f"{loss_components['neumann']:.2e}"
            })

    # --- Finalization ---
    training_time = time.time() - start_time
    print(f"\nTraining finished in {training_time:.2f} seconds.")

    # Store the final trained parameters for evaluation and plotting
    current_params_for_eval = params
    ```

 The implementation uses an MLP architecture with 5 hidden layers of 64 neurons each and tanh activation, taking normalized inputs (t, x scaled to [-1, 1] inside the forward method). Proposed name:KdV_Mixed_AnalyticalIC\Question :
We are solving a forward problem for the Korteweg-De Vries (KdV) equation:

$$
u_t + 6uu_x + u_{xxx} = 0
$$

**Domain:**
- Spatial: $x \in [0, 20]$
- Temporal: $t \in [0, 5]$

**Boundary Conditions:**

$$
u(0,x) = g_0(x) \quad 	ext{(Initial Condition)}
$$

$$
u(t,0) = g_1(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=0	ext{)}
$$

$$
u(t,20) = g_2(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

$$
u_x(t,20) = g_3(t) \quad 	ext{(Neumann BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

**Analytical Solution:**

The solution is given by a two-soliton interaction formula:

$$
ight]^2}osh\left(rac{\sqrt{c_1}\zeta_1 - \sqrt{c_2}\zeta_2}{2}_2}\zeta_2}{2}
$$


where:
- $\zeta_1 = x - c_1 t - x_1$
- $\zeta_2 = x - c_2 t - x_2$

**Parameters:**
- $c_1 = 6.0$ (velocity of first soliton)
- $c_2 = 2.0$ (velocity of second soliton)
- $x_1 = -2.0$ (initial position of first soliton)
- $x_2 = 2.0$ (initial position of second soliton)

The boundary conditions $g_0, g_1, g_2, g_3$ are derived by evaluating the analytical solution at the appropriate boundaries.

--- Running Debugging Team ---
Debugger Status: REJECTED_FATAL
Debugger Analysis: **Error Location:** Cell 4, Line 183

**Bug Type:** Incorrect Argument Passing to Activation Function

**Root Cause:**
The error occurs in the `MLP.__call__` method at line 183:
```python
X = self.activation(X)
```

The traceback reveals:
```
TypeError: Error interpreting argument to <function tanh at 0x7fcd9c9c7920> as an abstract array. The problematic value is of type <class '__main__.Args'> and was passed to the function at path args[0].
```

**Diagnosis:**
The model is being initialized at line 196 with:
```python
params = model.init(args.key, dummy_t, dummy_x)['params']
```

However, the `MLP` class definition at line 162-164 shows:
```python
class MLP(nn.Module):
    features: Sequence[int]
    activation: Callable = nn.tanh
```

The problem is that when the model is instantiated at line 189:
```python
model = MLP(features=args.layers, activation=args.activation)
```

The `args.activation` (which is `nn.tanh` from line 55) is being stored as a Flax module attribute. During the `model.init()` call, JAX's tracing mechanism is attempting to trace through the activation function, but it's receiving the `Args` class instance instead of the expected array `X`.

**The Core Issue:**
This is a **namespace collision**. The `self.activation` attribute is somehow being confused with the `args` object during JAX tracing. This typically happens when:
1. The activation function reference is not being properly bound to the module
2. There's a conflict in how Flax is interpreting the `activation` attribute during `@nn.compact` execution

**Fix Required:**
The Planner must modify the `MLP` class to ensure the activation function is correctly applied. The recommended fix is to either:
1. **Option A (Preferred):** Use the activation function directly without storing it as a module attribute:
   ```python
   class MLP(nn.Module):
       features: Sequence[int]
       
       @nn.compact
       def __call__(self, t, x):
           # ... normalization code ...
           for i, units in enumerate(self.features[:-1]):
               X = nn.Dense(features=units)(X)
               X = nn.tanh(X)  # Direct call
           X = nn.Dense(features=self.features[-1])(X)
           return X
   ```

2. **Option B:** Store the activation as a static field and call it correctly:
   ```python
   activation: Callable = nn.tanh
   
   @nn.compact
   def __call__(self, t, x):
       # ... normalization code ...
       act_fn = self.activation  # Bind locally
       for i, units in enumerate(self.features[:-1]):
           X = nn.Dense(features=units)(X)
           X = act_fn(X)
   ```

**Action for Planner:**
Rewrite the `MLP` class in Cell 4 to eliminate the activation function attribute confusion. The simplest solution is to hardcode `nn.tanh` directly in the forward pass, as the user's request does not require a configurable activation function.

    ```python
        features: Sequence[int]
    
        @nn.compact
        def __call__(self, t, x):
            # --- Input Normalization Layer ---
            # Scale t from [t_min, t_max] to [-1, 1]
            t_min, t_max = args.t_domain
            t_norm = 2.0 * (t - t_min) / (t_max - t_min) - 1.0
    
            # Scale x from [x_min, x_max] to [-1, 1]
            x_min, x_max = args.x_domain
            x_norm = 2.0 * (x - x_min) / (x_max - x_min) - 1.0
    
            # Concatenate normalized inputs
            X = jnp.hstack([t_norm, x_norm])
    
            # --- MLP Layers ---
            for i, units in enumerate(self.features[:-1]):
                X = nn.Dense(features=units)(X)
                X = nn.tanh(X)
    
            # Final layer (no activation)
            X = nn.Dense(features=self.features[-1])(X)
            return X
    ```

This indicates the model's final state is worse than its best state during training and that the overall accuracy is extremely poor.

The model has not learned the wave propagation, interaction, or amplitudes. The `Pointwise Error` plot confirms this, showing massive errors (order 10^0) precisely where the solitons should be.
    *   **PDE Residual:** The PDE residual plot shows large areas of low residual (blue). This is highly misleading. The high residual areas are likely artifacts near the boundaries where the trivial solution conflicts with the boundary conditions.

*   **Diagnosis:** **Catastrophic Underfitting & Convergence to a Trivial Solution.**

By predicting $u(x,t) \approx 0$, it can easily satisfy the PDE term ($0_t + 6(0)0_x + 0_{xxx} = 0$), which has a weight of 1.0 and is evaluated on 10,000 collocation points. The boundary and initial conditions, despite having a higher weight of 10.0, are evaluated on far fewer points and were not influential enough to force the network away from this trivial solution. The information from the boundaries failed to propagate into the domain's interior.

*   **Targeted Improvements:**
    *   **For Integrity:** The run was stable, so no integrity fixes are needed.
    *   **For Accuracy:** The immediate priority is to force the model away from the trivial solution.
        *   **Drastically Increase Boundary/Initial Condition Weights:** The current weights of 10 for IC/BC are insufficient. Increase them by at least an order of magnitude (e.g., `lambda_IC = 100`, `lambda_BC = 100`). The model must be heavily penalized for not respecting the known physical constraints.
        *   **Implement a Learning Rate Annealing Schedule:** Consider a more aggressive learning rate schedule or a two-phase training. For example, use a higher learning rate initially to escape local minima, then decay it to fine-tune the solution.
        *   **Curriculum Learning:** A powerful strategy here would be to first train the network for ~5k-10k iterations *only* on the IC and BC loss terms. This forces the model to first learn the solution at the boundaries. Then, introduce the PDE loss term for the remainder of the training to enforce the physics in the interior.
    *   **For Details:** Not applicable, as the model failed to capture even the coarse-grained behavior.
    *   **For Optimality:** The standard MLP architecture has known limitations (spectral bias) for problems with sharp gradients like solitons.
        *   **Introduce Fourier Feature Mapping:** Pre-process the input coordinates $(t, x)$ with a sinusoidal mapping (Fourier features). This helps the network learn high-frequency functions much more effectively and is a common technique for improving PINN performance on wave-like problems.

--- Strategy Report Received ---
To the Planner:

We are solving the Korteweg-De Vries (KdV) equation. The previous attempt failed because the model converged to the trivial solution $u(x,t)=0$, which satisfies the PDE but not the boundary conditions. To fix this, we will implement a **Fourier Feature Network** to handle the wave dynamics and a **Curriculum Learning** strategy to anchor the boundary conditions before enforcing the physics.

### 1. Data Source & Problem Statement

**Governing Equation:**
The PDE residual $f$ to be minimized is:
$$f = u_t + 6 u u_x + u_{xxx}$$
*   Note: The coefficients are $1.0$ for $u_t$, $6.0$ for the nonlinear term, and $1.0$ for the dispersive term.

**Domain:**
*   $t \in [0, 5]$
*   $x \in [0, 20]$

**Boundary/Initial Conditions (Data Generation):**
You must implement the exact analytical solution to generate the training data for the boundaries ($g_0, g_1, g_2, g_3$) and for validation.

**Analytical Solution Implementation (JAX):**
Implement this exact function to generate ground truth:
```python
def exact_solution(t, x):
    # Parameters
    c1 = 6.0
    c2 = 2.0
    x1 = -2.0
    x2 = 2.0
    
    # Intermediate variables
    sqrt_c1 = jnp.sqrt(c1)
    sqrt_c2 = jnp.sqrt(c2)
    zeta1 = x - c1 * t - x1
    zeta2 = x - c2 * t - x2
    
    # Terms
    term1 = c1 * jnp.cosh(sqrt_c2 * zeta2 / 2)**2
    term2 = c2 * jnp.sinh(sqrt_c1 * zeta1 / 2)**2
    numerator = 2 * (c1 - c2) * (term1 + term2)
    
    denom_term1 = (sqrt_c1 - sqrt_c2) * jnp.cosh((sqrt_c1 * zeta1 + sqrt_c2 * zeta2) / 2)
    denom_term2 = (sqrt_c1 + sqrt_c2) * jnp.cosh((sqrt_c1 * zeta1 - sqrt_c2 * zeta2) / 2)
    denominator = (denom_term1 + denom_term2)**2
    
    u = numerator / denominator
    return u
```

**Training Data Sampling:**
1.  **Collocation Points ($N_f=10,000$):** Sample $(t, x)$ inside the domain using Latin Hypercube Sampling (LHS). **Do not** use 'maximin' optimization for LHS.
2.  **Initial Condition ($N_{ic}=500$):** Sample $x \in [0, 20]$ at $t=0$. Target $u = \text{exact\_solution}(0, x)$.
3.  **Boundary Left ($N_{b1}=500$):** Sample $t \in [0, 5]$ at $x=0$. Target $u = \text{exact\_solution}(t, 0)$.
4.  **Boundary Right ($N_{b2}=500$):** Sample $t \in [0, 5]$ at $x=20$. Target $u = \text{exact\_solution}(t, 20)$.
5.  **Neumann Right ($N_{b3}=500$):** Sample $t \in [0, 5]$ at $x=20$. Target $u_x = \partial_x \text{exact\_solution}(t, x)|_{x=20}$. *Note: You will need to use `jax.grad` or `jax.jvp` on the exact solution function to get the target gradient.*

### 2. Model Architecture

**Main Model:** **Fourier Feature MLP**
Standard MLPs struggle with high-frequency wave components. We will map the inputs to a higher-dimensional feature space before passing them to the MLP.

1.  **Input:** $(t, x)$ (Shape: `[batch, 2]`)
2.  **Fourier Embedding Layer:**
    *   Create a fixed weight matrix $B$ of shape `[2, m]` (e.g., $m=20$), sampled from a Gaussian distribution $\mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)$. Set $\sigma=1.0$.
    *   Compute features: $v = [ \sin(2\pi \mathbf{x} B), \cos(2\pi \mathbf{x} B) ]$.
    *   Concatenate these to form the input to the MLP.
3.  **Backbone:**
    *   4 hidden layers.
    *   64 neurons per layer.
    *   Activation: `tanh`.
4.  **Output:**
    *   1 neuron ($u$).

### 3. Optimization & Loss Function

**Optimizer:** Adam (via Optax).
**Total Iterations:** 50,000.

**Strategy: Curriculum Learning**
To prevent the trivial solution ($u=0$), we will first train the network to satisfy the boundary conditions, then introduce the PDE residual.

**Phase 1: Boundary Fitting (Iterations 0 - 5,000)**
*   **Loss:** $\mathcal{L}_{Phase1} = \mathcal{L}_{IC} + \mathcal{L}_{BC\_Left} + \mathcal{L}_{BC\_Right} + \mathcal{L}_{Neumann}$
*   **Learning Rate:** $1 \times 10^{-3}$ (constant).
*   **Goal:** Force the network to learn the shape of the solution at the boundaries.

**Phase 2: Physics Enforcement (Iterations 5,001 - 50,000)**
*   **Loss:** $\mathcal{L}_{Total} = \mathcal{L}_{PDE} + \lambda (\mathcal{L}_{IC} + \mathcal{L}_{BC\_Left} + \mathcal{L}_{BC\_Right} + \mathcal{L}_{Neumann})$
*   **Weights:** Set $\lambda = 100.0$. This high weight is critical to maintain the boundary shape while minimizing the PDE residual.
*   **Learning Rate:** Exponential decay starting at $1 \times 10^{-3}$ and decaying to $1 \times 10^{-5}$.

**Loss Terms Definition:**
*   $\mathcal{L}_{PDE} = \frac{1}{N_f} \sum (u_t + 6uu_x + u_{xxx})^2$
*   $\mathcal{L}_{IC} = \frac{1}{N_{ic}} \sum (u_{pred} - u_{exact})^2$
*   $\mathcal{L}_{BC} = \frac{1}{N_{b}} \sum (u_{pred} - u_{exact})^2$ (for Dirichlet BCs)
*   $\mathcal{L}_{Neumann} = \frac{1}{N_{b3}} \sum (\partial_x u_{pred} - \partial_x u_{exact})^2$

### 4. Output Requirements
*   **Main Model:** Fourier Feature MLP with Optax Adam.
*   **Enhancements:** Curriculum Learning (2-phase training), High BC weights ($\lambda=100$).
*   **Reporting:** Calculate and print the Loss and Relative L2 Error against the exact solution on a validation grid ($256 \times 100$) every **500** iterations.

### Periodic_Fourier_Features
```python
import flax.linen as nn
import jax
import jax.numpy as jnp

class Periodic_Fourier_Features(nn.Module):
    """
    A layer that maps input coordinates to a higher-dimensional space
    using periodic Fourier features. This helps the network learn
    high-frequency functions more effectively.

    Attributes:
        features (int): The number of Fourier features (m in the paper). The
                        output dimension will be 2 * features.
        sigma (float): The standard deviation of the Gaussian distribution for
                       the random frequency matrix B.
    """
    features: int
    sigma: float = 1.0

    def setup(self):
        """
        Initializes the random frequency matrix B, which is not trained.
        """
        # The input dimension is assumed to be 2 (e.g., t, x).
        input_dim = 2
        key = self.make_rng('params')
        # B matrix of shape [input_dim, features]
        self.B = jax.random.normal(key, (input_dim, self.features)) * self.sigma

    def __call__(self, x):
        """
        Applies the Fourier feature mapping to the input.

        Args:
            x (jax.numpy.ndarray): The input tensor of shape [batch, input_dim].

        Returns:
            jax.numpy.ndarray: The mapped tensor of shape [batch, 2 * features].
        """
        # Project the input onto the random directions
        x_proj = 2 * jnp.pi * x @ self.B
        # Apply sine and cosine functions
        return jnp.concatenate([jnp.sin(x_proj), jnp.cos(x_proj)], axis=-1)
```

*   **Action:** Replace the entire cell content. The `MLP` class is replaced with the `Periodic_Fourier_Features` class from the reference functions and a new `FourierFeaturePINN` model that incorporates it, as required by the scientific strategy. The model instantiation is updated accordingly.
*   **New Code:**
    ```python
    class Periodic_Fourier_Features(nn.Module):
        """
        A layer that maps input coordinates to a higher-dimensional space
        using periodic Fourier features. This helps the network learn
        high-frequency functions more effectively.
    
        Attributes:
            features (int): The number of Fourier features (m in the paper). The
                            output dimension will be 2 * features.
            sigma (float): The standard deviation of the Gaussian distribution for
                           the random frequency matrix B.
        """
        features: int
        sigma: float = 1.0
    
        def setup(self):
            """
            Initializes the random frequency matrix B, which is not trained.
            """
            # The input dimension is assumed to be 2 (e.g., t, x).
            input_dim = 2
            key = self.make_rng('params')
            # B matrix of shape [input_dim, features]
            self.B = jax.random.normal(key, (input_dim, self.features)) * self.sigma
    
        def __call__(self, x):
            """
            Applies the Fourier feature mapping to the input.
    
            Args:
                x (jax.numpy.ndarray): The input tensor of shape [batch, input_dim].
    
            Returns:
                jax.numpy.ndarray: The mapped tensor of shape [batch, 2 * features].
            """
            # Project the input onto the random directions
            x_proj = 2 * jnp.pi * x @ self.B
            # Apply sine and cosine functions
            return jnp.concatenate([jnp.sin(x_proj), jnp.cos(x_proj)], axis=-1)
    
    
    class FourierFeaturePINN(nn.Module):
        features: Sequence[int]
    
        @nn.compact
        def __call__(self, t, x):
            # --- Input Normalization Layer ---
            t_min, t_max = args.t_domain
            t_norm = 2.0 * (t - t_min) / (t_max - t_min) - 1.0
            x_min, x_max = args.x_domain
            x_norm = 2.0 * (x - x_min) / (x_max - x_min) - 1.0
            X_norm = jnp.hstack([t_norm, x_norm])
    
            # --- Fourier Feature Layer ---
            X = Periodic_Fourier_Features(
                features=args.fourier_features,
                sigma=args.fourier_sigma
            )(X_norm)
    
            # --- MLP Layers ---
            for i, units in enumerate(self.features[:-1]):
                X = nn.Dense(features=units)(X)
                X = args.activation(X)
    
            # Final layer (no activation)
            X = nn.Dense(features=self.features[-1])(X)
            return X
    
    # Instantiate the model
    model = FourierFeaturePINN(features=args.layers)
    
    # Initialize parameters
    # Create a dummy input with the correct shape (N, 1) for t and x
    dummy_t = jnp.ones((1, 1))
    dummy_x = jnp.ones((1, 1))
    params = model.init(args.key, dummy_t, dummy_x)['params']
    
    # Define the model's apply function for predictions
    apply_fn = jit(model.apply)
    print(f"Model initialized with {sum(p.size for p in jax.tree_util.tree_leaves(params))} parameters.")
    ```

--- Analizing: # ## Cell 5: Optimizer Setup ---
  - [Planner]: Instructions received:
### [CURRENT_CELL_ID]
*   **Action:** Replace the entire cell content to implement the two-phase curriculum learning strategy. This involves creating separate loss functions (`loss_fn_phase1`, `loss_fn_phase2`), optimizers (`optimizer_phase1`, `optimizer_phase2`), and JIT-compiled training steps (`train_step_phase1`, `train_step_phase2`) for each phase.
*   **New Code:**
    ```python
    # --- PDE Residual Function (Unchanged) ---
    
    def PDE_residuals(params, t, x, apply_fn):
        """
        Computes the residual of the KdV equation: f = u_t + 6*u*u_x + u_xxx
        """
        def u_scalar(t_s, x_s):
            return apply_fn({'params': params}, t_s.reshape(1, -1), x_s.reshape(1, -1))[0, 0]
    
        u_t_fn = jax.grad(u_scalar, argnums=0)
        u_x_fn = jax.grad(u_scalar, argnums=1)
        u_xx_fn = jax.grad(u_x_fn, argnums=1)
        u_xxx_fn = jax.grad(u_xx_fn, argnums=1)
    
        u_t_vmap = vmap(u_t_fn)
        u_x_vmap = vmap(u_x_fn)
        u_xxx_vmap = vmap(u_xxx_fn)
    
        u_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t, x)
        u_t = u_t_vmap(t.flatten(), x.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
        u_x = u_x_vmap(t.flatten(), x.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
        u_xxx = u_xxx_vmap(t.flatten(), x.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
    
        residual = u_t + 6.0 * u_pred * u_x + u_xxx
        return residual
    
    # --- Loss Functions for Curriculum Learning ---
    
    @jit
    def loss_fn_phase1(params):
        """Loss for Phase 1: Data-driven (IC/BC only)."""
        # 1. Initial Condition Loss
        u_ic_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_ic, x_ic)
        loss_ic = jnp.mean((u_ic_pred - u_ic)**2)
    
        # 2. Boundary Condition Losses (Dirichlet)
        u_bc1_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_bc1, x_bc1)  # Left
        u_bc2_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_bc2, x_bc2)  # Right
        loss_bc = jnp.mean((u_bc1_pred - u_bc1)**2) + jnp.mean((u_bc2_pred - u_bc2)**2)
    
        # 3. Neumann Boundary Condition Loss
        def u_scalar_for_grad(t_s, x_s):
            return apply_fn({'params': params}, t_s.reshape(1,-1), x_s.reshape(1,-1))[0, 0]
        u_x_model_fn = vmap(jax.grad(u_scalar_for_grad, argnums=1))
        u_x_bc3_pred = u_x_model_fn(t_bc3.flatten(), x_bc3.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
        loss_neumann = jnp.mean((u_x_bc3_pred - u_x_bc3)**2)
    
        total_loss = loss_ic + loss_bc + loss_neumann
        loss_components = {"total": total_loss, "pde": 0.0, "ic": loss_ic, "bc": loss_bc, "neumann": loss_neumann}
        return total_loss, loss_components
    
    @jit
    def loss_fn_phase2(params):
        """Loss for Phase 2: Physics-informed (PDE + weighted data)."""
        # 1. PDE Loss
        f_pred = PDE_residuals(params, t_f, x_f, apply_fn)
        loss_pde = jnp.mean(f_pred**2)
    
        # 2. Initial Condition Loss
        u_ic_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_ic, x_ic)
        loss_ic = jnp.mean((u_ic_pred - u_ic)**2)
    
        # 3. Boundary Condition Losses (Dirichlet)
        u_bc1_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_bc1, x_bc1)  # Left
        u_bc2_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_bc2, x_bc2)  # Right
        loss_bc = jnp.mean((u_bc1_pred - u_bc1)**2) + jnp.mean((u_bc2_pred - u_bc2)**2)
    
        # 4. Neumann Boundary Condition Loss
        def u_scalar_for_grad(t_s, x_s):
            return apply_fn({'params': params}, t_s.reshape(1,-1), x_s.reshape(1,-1))[0, 0]
        u_x_model_fn = vmap(jax.grad(u_scalar_for_grad, argnums=1))
        u_x_bc3_pred = u_x_model_fn(t_bc3.flatten(), x_bc3.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
        loss_neumann = jnp.mean((u_x_bc3_pred - u_x_bc3)**2)
    
        loss_data = loss_ic + loss_bc + loss_neumann
        total_loss = args.lambda_pde * loss_pde + args.lambda_data * loss_data
        loss_components = {"total": total_loss, "pde": loss_pde, "ic": loss_ic, "bc": loss_bc, "neumann": loss_neumann}
        return total_loss, loss_components
    
    # --- Optimizer Setup for Each Phase ---
    
    # Optimizer for Phase 1 (constant learning rate)
    optimizer_phase1 = optax.adam(learning_rate=args.learning_rate_phase1)
    opt_state = optimizer_phase1.init(params) # Initial state
    
    # Optimizer for Phase 2 (decaying learning rate)
    total_steps_phase2 = args.iterations - args.curriculum_milestone
    lr_schedule_phase2 = optax.exponential_decay(
        init_value=args.learning_rate_phase2_start,
        transition_steps=1000, # Decay every 1000 steps
        decay_rate=0.9, # A common decay rate
    )
    optimizer_phase2 = optax.adam(learning_rate=lr_schedule_phase2)
    
    # --- JIT-Compiled Training Steps for Each Phase ---
    
    @jit
    def train_step_phase1(params, opt_state):
        (loss_val, loss_components), grads = value_and_grad(loss_fn_phase1, has_aux=True)(params)
        updates, opt_state = optimizer_phase1.update(grads, opt_state, params)
        params = optax.apply_updates(params, updates)
        return params, opt_state, loss_val, loss_components
    
    @jit
    def train_step_phase2(params, opt_state):
        (loss_val, loss_components), grads = value_and_grad(loss_fn_phase2, has_aux=True)(params)
        updates, opt_state = optimizer_phase2.update(grads, opt_state, params)
        params = optax.apply_updates(params, updates)
        return params, opt_state, loss_val, loss_components
    
    print("Optimizers and curriculum-based training steps initialized.")
    ```

--- Analizing: # ## Cell 6: Training ---
  - [Planner]: Instructions received:
### [CURRENT_CELL_ID]
*   **Action:** Replace the main training loop (lines 1-53) to implement the two-phase curriculum learning strategy. This involves a conditional check on the iteration number to switch between `train_step_phase1` and `train_step_phase2`. It also re-initializes the optimizer state when switching to Phase 2.
*   **New Code:**
    ```python
    # --- Main Training Loop ---
    print("Starting training with curriculum learning...")
    start_time = time.time()
    
    # Lists to store metrics
    all_its = []
    all_loss = []
    all_errors = []
    all_L_2 = []
    all_L_infty = []
    
    # Use tqdm for a progress bar
    pbar = tqdm(range(args.iterations), desc="Phase 1: Data Fitting")
    
    for it in pbar:
        # --- Curriculum Learning Logic ---
        if it < args.curriculum_milestone:
            # Phase 1: Train on IC/BC data only
            params, opt_state, loss_val, loss_components = train_step_phase1(params, opt_state)
        else:
            # Phase 2: Switch to physics-informed training
            if it == args.curriculum_milestone:
                print("\nSwitching to Phase 2: Physics Enforcement")
                # Re-initialize optimizer state for the new optimizer
                opt_state = optimizer_phase2.init(params)
                pbar.set_description("Phase 2: Physics")
    
            params, opt_state, loss_val, loss_components = train_step_phase2(params, opt_state)
    
        # --- Reporting and Error Calculation ---
        if it % args.report_every == 0 or it == args.iterations - 1:
            # Predict solution on the validation grid
            u_pred_val = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_val.reshape(-1, 1), x_val.reshape(-1, 1))
            u_pred_val = u_pred_val.reshape(Exact.shape)
    
            # Calculate relative L2 error
            error_l2 = jnp.linalg.norm(u_pred_val - Exact) / jnp.linalg.norm(Exact)
            
            # Calculate L-infinity error
            error_linf = jnp.max(jnp.abs(u_pred_val - Exact))
    
            # Store metrics
            all_its.append(it)
            all_loss.append(loss_val.item())
            all_errors.append(error_l2.item())
            all_L_2.append(jnp.linalg.norm(u_pred_val - Exact).item())
            all_L_infty.append(error_linf.item())
    
            # Update progress bar description
            pbar.set_postfix({
                "Loss": f"{loss_val.item():.4e}",
                "L2 Error": f"{error_l2.item():.4e}",
                "PDE": f"{loss_components['pde']:.2e}",
                "IC": f"{loss_components['ic']:.2e}",
                "BC": f"{loss_components['bc']:.2e}",
                "Neumann": f"{loss_components['neumann']:.2e}"
            })
    
    # --- Finalization ---
    training_time = time.time() - start_time
    print(f"\nTraining finished in {training_time:.2f} seconds.")
    
    # Store the final trained parameters for evaluation and plotting
    current_params_for_eval = params
    ```

Inspector summary:
 Fourier Feature MLP architecture with input embedding via Periodic Fourier Features (m=20, sigma=1.0) on normalized (t,x) inputs to [-1,1], followed by 4 hidden layers of 64 neurons each using tanh activation, and 1 output neuron for u. Optimizer is Optax Adam with curriculum learning: Phase 1 (iterations 0-5000) uses constant LR=1e-3 on L_IC + L_BC_left + L_BC_right + L_Neumann (all MSE against exact u or u_x); Phase 2 (5001-50000) uses exponential decay LR from 1e-3 (transition_steps=1000, decay_rate=0.9) on L_total = 1.0 * L_PDE + 100.0 * (L_IC + L_BC_left + L_BC_right + L_Neumann), where L_PDE is MSE of (u_t + 6 u u_x + u_xxx). Reports relative L2 error on 256x100 validation grid every 500 iterations.
Proposed name:KdV_Mixed_AnalyticalIC\Question :
We are solving a forward problem for the Korteweg-De Vries (KdV) equation:

$$
u_t + 6uu_x + u_{xxx} = 0
$$

**Domain:**
- Spatial: $x \in [0, 20]$
- Temporal: $t \in [0, 5]$

**Boundary Conditions:**

$$
u(0,x) = g_0(x) \quad 	ext{(Initial Condition)}
$$

$$
u(t,0) = g_1(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=0	ext{)}
$$

$$
u(t,20) = g_2(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

$$
u_x(t,20) = g_3(t) \quad 	ext{(Neumann BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

**Analytical Solution:**

The solution is given by a two-soliton interaction formula:

$$
ight]^2}osh\left(rac{\sqrt{c_1}\zeta_1 - \sqrt{c_2}\zeta_2}{2}_2}\zeta_2}{2}
$$


where:
- $\zeta_1 = x - c_1 t - x_1$
- $\zeta_2 = x - c_2 t - x_2$

**Parameters:**
- $c_1 = 6.0$ (velocity of first soliton)
- $c_2 = 2.0$ (velocity of second soliton)
- $x_1 = -2.0$ (initial position of first soliton)
- $x_2 = 2.0$ (initial position of second soliton)

The boundary conditions $g_0, g_1, g_2, g_3$ are derived by evaluating the analytical solution at the appropriate boundaries.

MANDATORY: Make sure to report the loss and errors every 500 iterations and you cannonly use optimizers that work natively in JAX (NO SciPY)
CONSTRAINTS: If using latin hypercube sampling dont use any criterion such as 'maximin'.

```python
activation = nn.tanh
```
to:
```python
activation = jax.nn.tanh
```
Alternatively, if using Flax's activation, Line 232 should be:
```python
X = nn.tanh(X)
```
(calling the module directly, not via `args.activation`). The cleanest solution is to use `jax.nn.tanh` in the Args class, as it's a pure function compatible with JAX tracing.

- $c_1 = 6.0$ (velocity of first soliton)
- $c_2 = 2.0$ (velocity of second soliton)
- $x_1 = -2.0$ (initial position of first soliton)
- $x_2 = 2.0$ (initial position of second soliton)

The boundary conditions $g_0, g_1, g_2, g_3$ are derived by evaluating the analytical solution at the appropriate boundaries.

Debugger Status: REJECTED_FATAL
Debugger Analysis: **Error Location:** Cell 4, Line 223

**Bug Analysis:**

The log shows a `TypeError` at Line 223 in the `FourierFeaturePINN.__call__` method:

```python
X = args.activation(X)
```

The error message states:
> "Error interpreting argument to <function tanh at 0x7f40182af880> as an abstract array. The problematic value is of type <class '__main__.Args'> and was passed to the function at path args[0]."

**Root Cause:**

This is a **variable name collision**. The model's `__call__` method has two parameters: `(self, t, x)`. However, JAX's `jit` compilation is interpreting the *global* `args` object (the hyperparameter configuration class) as the *first positional argument* to the activation function, instead of the local variable `X`.

The issue arises because:
1. Line 223 references `args.activation(X)`, where `args` is a global object.
2. During JAX tracing, the function signature `model.apply({'params': params}, t, x)` is being analyzed.
3. JAX's error message "passed to the function at path args[0]" indicates it's confusing the global `args` with a function argument.

**The Fix:**

The Planner must refactor the code to avoid this collision. Two solutions:

**Option 1 (Recommended):** Store the activation function as a class attribute during model initialization:
```python
class FourierFeaturePINN(nn.Module):
    features: Sequence[int]
    activation: Callable  # Add this line

    @nn.compact
    def __call__(self, t, x):
        # ... (rest of code)
        X = self.activation(X)  # Change Line 223 to use self.activation
```

Then instantiate the model with:
```python
model = FourierFeaturePINN(features=args.layers, activation=args.activation)
```

**Option 2:** Use a local variable:
```python
@nn.compact
def __call__(self, t, x):
    activation_fn = jax.nn.tanh  # Hardcode or pass as argument
    # ...
    X = activation_fn(X)  # Line 223
```

**Summary:**
This is a **Planner Error** (fixable). The code has a namespace collision between the global `args` object and JAX's function tracing mechanism. The Planner must modify Cell 4 (Lines 202-227) to eliminate the reference to `args.activation` inside the JIT-compiled model.

    ```python
    class FourierFeaturePINN(nn.Module):
        features: Sequence[int]
        activation: Callable

        @nn.compact
        def __call__(self, t, x):
            # --- Input Normalization Layer ---
            t_min, t_max = args.t_domain
            t_norm = 2.0 * (t - t_min) / (t_max - t_min) - 1.0
            x_min, x_max = args.x_domain
            x_norm = 2.0 * (x - x_min) / (x_max - x_min) - 1.0
            X_norm = jnp.hstack([t_norm, x_norm])

            # --- Fourier Feature Layer ---
            X = Periodic_Fourier_Features(
                features=args.fourier_features,
                sigma=args.fourier_sigma
            )(X_norm)

            # --- MLP Layers ---
            for i, units in enumerate(self.features[:-1]):
                X = nn.Dense(features=units)(X)
                X = self.activation(X)

            # Final layer (no activation)
            X = nn.Dense(features=self.features[-1])(X)
            return X
    ```

We are solving a forward problem for the Korteweg-De Vries (KdV) equation:

$$
u_t + 6uu_x + u_{xxx} = 0
$$

**Domain:**
- Spatial: $x \in [0, 20]$
- Temporal: $t \in [0, 5]$

**Boundary Conditions:**

$$
u(0,x) = g_0(x) \quad 	ext{(Initial Condition)}
$$

$$
u(t,0) = g_1(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=0	ext{)}
$$

$$
u(t,20) = g_2(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

$$
u_x(t,20) = g_3(t) \quad 	ext{(Neumann BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

**Analytical Solution:**

The solution is given by a two-soliton interaction formula:

$$
ight]^2}osh\left(rac{\sqrt{c_1}\zeta_1 - \sqrt{c_2}\zeta_2}{2}_2}\zeta_2}{2}
$$


where:
- $\zeta_1 = x - c_1 t - x_1$
- $\zeta_2 = x - c_2 t - x_2$

**Parameters:**
- $c_1 = 6.0$ (velocity of first soliton)
- $c_2 = 2.0$ (velocity of second soliton)
- $x_1 = -2.0$ (initial position of first soliton)
- $x_2 = 2.0$ (initial position of second soliton)

The boundary conditions $g_0, g_1, g_2, g_3$ are derived by evaluating the analytical solution at the appropriate boundaries.

1. Passed as static arguments (marked with `static_argnums` in a `jax.jit` wrapper), or
2. Stored as class attributes during `__init__` (for Flax modules), or
3. Computed outside the JIT boundary and passed as arrays.

Debugger Analysis: A TypeError occurred in **Cell 4, Line 218** during model initialization. The error message indicates: 'Error interpreting argument to <function tanh at 0x7fe60f397880> as an abstract array. The problematic value is of type <class '__main__.Args'> and was passed to the function at path args[0].' This is a classic JAX error that occurs when a non-array value (in this case, the 'Args' class instance) is passed to a jit-compiled function. The root cause is in the model definition at Line 218: 'X = self.activation(X)'. The issue is that 'self.activation' is set to 'args.activation' (Line 224), which is 'jax.nn.tanh'. However, during the model's '__call__' method, JAX is tracing the computation and expects all inputs to be arrays or static arguments. The 'self.activation' attribute is being treated as a dynamic value, but it should be a static function reference. The Planner must fix this by ensuring that the activation function is either: (1) directly referenced as 'jax.nn.tanh' inside the model's '__call__' method instead of using 'self.activation', or (2) the activation function is passed as a static argument if using '@jit' decorators. The current implementation incorrectly stores a function reference as a module attribute, which JAX cannot handle during tracing. This is a fixable bug in the model architecture definition.

Inspector summary:
 The implementation uses a Fourier Feature MLP architecture: inputs (t,x) are normalized to [-1,1] inside the forward method, mapped via Periodic Fourier Features (m=20, sigma=1.0) producing 40D features, followed by 4 hidden layers of 64 neurons each with tanh activation, and 1 output for u. Optimization employs Optax Adam with a two-phase curriculum learning strategy: Phase 1 (iterations 0-5000) uses constant LR=1e-3 and loss L_total = L_IC + L_BC_Left + L_BC_Right + L_Neumann (all MSE against exact); Phase 2 (5001-50000) switches to exponential decay LR schedule (init=1e-3, decay_rate=0.9 every 1000 steps toward ~1e-5) and loss L_total = 1.0 * L_PDE + 100.0 * (L_IC + L_BC_Left + L_BC_Right + L_Neumann), where L_PDE is MSE of (u_t + 6*u*u_x + u_xxx). Training reports relative L2 error on 256x100 validation grid every 500 iterations.
Proposed name:KdV_Mixed_AnalyticalIC\Question :
We are solving a forward problem for the Korteweg-De Vries (KdV) equation:

$$
u_t + 6uu_x + u_{xxx} = 0
$$

**Domain:**
- Spatial: $x \in [0, 20]$
- Temporal: $t \in [0, 5]$

**Boundary Conditions:**

$$
u(0,x) = g_0(x) \quad 	ext{(Initial Condition)}
$$

$$
u(t,0) = g_1(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=0	ext{)}
$$

$$
u(t,20) = g_2(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

$$
u_x(t,20) = g_3(t) \quad 	ext{(Neumann BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

**Analytical Solution:**

The solution is given by a two-soliton interaction formula:

$$
ight]^2}osh\left(rac{\sqrt{c_1}\zeta_1 - \sqrt{c_2}\zeta_2}{2}_2}\zeta_2}{2}
$$


where:
- $\zeta_1 = x - c_1 t - x_1$
- $\zeta_2 = x - c_2 t - x_2$

**Parameters:**
- $c_1 = 6.0$ (velocity of first soliton)
- $c_2 = 2.0$ (velocity of second soliton)
- $x_1 = -2.0$ (initial position of first soliton)
- $x_2 = 2.0$ (initial position of second soliton)

The boundary conditions $g_0, g_1, g_2, g_3$ are derived by evaluating the analytical solution at the appropriate boundaries.

MANDATORY: Make sure to report the loss and errors every 500 iterations and you cannonly use optimizers that work natively in JAX (NO SciPY)
CONSTRAINTS: If using latin hypercube sampling dont use any criterion such as 'maximin'.

Debugger Analysis: The code failed with an **IndentationError** in **Cell 4 (Model Definition), specifically at Line 217**.

**Error Details:**
The log shows:
```
File "...KdV_Mixed_AnalyticalIC.py", line 217
    X = nn.Dense(features=self.features[-1])(X)
IndentationError: unexpected indent
```

**Root Cause:**
Looking at the code structure in Cell 4 (lines 212-218), there is a clear indentation inconsistency:

- **Line 213-214:** The `for` loop and its body (`X = nn.Dense(...)`) are correctly indented.
- **Line 215:** `X = nn.tanh(X)` is **incorrectly indented** at the same level as the `for` statement, when it should be inside the loop body.
- **Line 217:** `X = nn.Dense(features=self.features[-1])(X)` is indented at the same level as line 215, but Python interprets this as an unexpected continuation after the `for` loop has already been closed by the dedented line 215.

**The Bug:**
Line 215 should be indented to align with line 214 (inside the `for` loop). The current structure causes Python to think the loop ends at line 214, making line 217's indentation invalid.

**Correct Structure Should Be:**
```python
for i, units in enumerate(self.features[:-1]):
    X = nn.Dense(features=units)(X)
    X = nn.tanh(X)  # <-- This must be indented to be inside the loop
# Final layer (no activation)
X = nn.Dense(features=self.features[-1])(X)
```

**Fix Required:**
The Planner must correct the indentation of **Line 215** to match the indentation level of **Line 214** (4 additional spaces). This is a pure syntax error in the MLP layer construction within the `FourierFeaturePINN` model's `__call__` method.

--- Running Debugging Team ---
Debugger Status: REJECTED_FATAL
Debugger Analysis: The error occurred in **Cell 4, Line 179** during the initialization of the `Periodic_Fourier_Features` layer.

**Root Cause:**
The code attempts to call `self.make_rng('params')` inside the `setup()` method of the `Periodic_Fourier_Features` Flax module. However, the model was initialized without providing an RNG key for the 'params' collection. The error message explicitly states:

`flax.errors.InvalidRngError: Periodic_Fourier_Features_0 needs PRNG for "params"`

**Why This Happened:**
In **Line 231**, the model is initialized as:
```python
params = model.init(args.key, dummy_t, dummy_x)['params']
```

This only provides a single RNG key (`args.key`), which Flax assigns to the default 'params' collection. However, when a Flax module calls `self.make_rng('params')` *inside* `setup()` or `__call__()`, it requires that the RNG key be explicitly passed during initialization using the `rngs` argument.

**The Fix:**
The Planner must modify **Line 231** to provide the RNG key for the 'params' collection explicitly:

**Incorrect (Current):**
```python
params = model.init(args.key, dummy_t, dummy_x)['params']
```

**Correct:**
```python
params = model.init({'params': args.key}, dummy_t, dummy_x)['params']
```

This ensures that the 'params' RNG is available when `Periodic_Fourier_Features.setup()` calls `self.make_rng('params')` to generate the random frequency matrix `B`.

**Summary:**
This is a **Planner Error** in the model initialization logic. The fix is a one-line change to pass the RNG key correctly to `model.init()`.

Inspector summary:
 Fourier Feature MLP architecture with input normalization to [-1,1], Periodic Fourier Features (m=20, sigma=1.0) mapping to 40D, followed by 4 hidden layers of 64 neurons each using tanh activation, and 1 output for u. Optimizer is Optax Adam. Implements strict 2-phase Curriculum Learning: Phase 1 (iterations 0-4999) uses constant LR=1e-3 with loss L_IC + L_BC_Left + L_BC_Right + L_Neumann; Phase 2 (5000-49999) switches to exponential decay LR schedule (init=1e-3, transition_steps=1000, decay_rate=0.9) with loss = 1.0 * L_PDE + 100.0 * (L_IC + L_BC_Left + L_BC_Right + L_Neumann), where L_PDE = mean((u_t + 6*u*u_x + u_xxx)^2). Reports relative L2 error and losses every 500 iterations on 256x100 validation grid.
Proposed name:KdV_Mixed_AnalyticalIC\Question :
We are solving a forward problem for the Korteweg-De Vries (KdV) equation:

$$
u_t + 6uu_x + u_{xxx} = 0
$$

**Domain:**
- Spatial: $x \in [0, 20]$
- Temporal: $t \in [0, 5]$

**Boundary Conditions:**

$$
u(0,x) = g_0(x) \quad 	ext{(Initial Condition)}
$$

$$
u(t,0) = g_1(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=0	ext{)}
$$

$$
u(t,20) = g_2(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

$$
u_x(t,20) = g_3(t) \quad 	ext{(Neumann BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

**Analytical Solution:**

The solution is given by a two-soliton interaction formula:

$$
ight]^2}osh\left(rac{\sqrt{c_1}\zeta_1 - \sqrt{c_2}\zeta_2}{2}_2}\zeta_2}{2}
$$


where:
- $\zeta_1 = x - c_1 t - x_1$
- $\zeta_2 = x - c_2 t - x_2$

**Parameters:**
- $c_1 = 6.0$ (velocity of first soliton)
- $c_2 = 2.0$ (velocity of second soliton)
- $x_1 = -2.0$ (initial position of first soliton)
- $x_2 = 2.0$ (initial position of second soliton)

The boundary conditions $g_0, g_1, g_2, g_3$ are derived by evaluating the analytical solution at the appropriate boundaries.

Debugger Status: REJECTED_FATAL
Debugger Analysis: **Error Location:** Cell 4, Line 179

**Error Type:** `flax.errors.InvalidRngError`

**Root Cause Analysis:**
The error occurs in the `Periodic_Fourier_Features.setup()` method when it attempts to call `self.make_rng('params')`. This is a Flax-specific error indicating that the module was not provided with a PRNG key for the 'params' collection during initialization.

**The Bug:**
In **Cell 4, Line 231**, the model is initialized with:
```python
params = model.init({'params': args.key}, dummy_t, dummy_x)['params']
```

The problem is that `model.init()` expects a dictionary mapping RNG collection names to PRNG keys. The code is passing `{'params': args.key}`, which tells Flax that `args.key` is the RNG for the 'params' collection. However, Flax's convention is that the **first positional argument** to `init()` should be the RNG dictionary, and the standard collection name for initialization is `'params'`.

**However**, the `Periodic_Fourier_Features` module's `setup()` method (Line 179) calls `self.make_rng('params')`, which requires that an RNG key was provided for the 'params' collection. The issue is that when you pass `{'params': args.key}` to `model.init()`, Flax interprets this as the RNG for *parameter initialization*, but the `make_rng()` call inside `setup()` is trying to access a *separate* RNG stream for the 'params' collection during the forward pass.

**The Correct Fix:**
The initialization call should provide the RNG key in a way that Flax can use it for both parameter initialization AND for any `make_rng()` calls inside modules. The standard pattern is:

```python
params = model.init(args.key, dummy_t, dummy_x)['params']
```

OR, if you need explicit control over RNG collections:

```python
params = model.init({'params': args.key, 'dropout': another_key}, dummy_t, dummy_x)['params']
```

But in this case, since `Periodic_Fourier_Features` is calling `make_rng('params')`, the simplest fix is to change Line 231 to:

```python
params = model.init(args.key, dummy_t, dummy_x)['params']
```

This will allow Flax to automatically manage the RNG for the 'params' collection.

**Alternatively**, if the intent is to make the Fourier features deterministic (not random per call), the `Periodic_Fourier_Features.setup()` method should be modified to initialize `self.B` as a *parameter* or *variable*, not by calling `make_rng()` during setup. The current implementation is incorrect because `make_rng()` is meant to be called during the forward pass (in `__call__`), not in `setup()`.

**Recommended Action for Planner:**
1. Change Line 231 from `params = model.init({'params': args.key}, dummy_t, dummy_x)['params']` to `params = model.init(args.key, dummy_t, dummy_x)['params']`.
2. **OR** (better design): Modify the `Periodic_Fourier_Features.setup()` method to initialize `self.B` as a non-trainable parameter using `self.param()` with a custom initializer, rather than calling `self.make_rng('params')`. This is the standard Flax pattern for fixed random features.

**Error Message:** "Periodic_Fourier_Features_0 needs PRNG for 'params'"

**Root Cause:**
The `Periodic_Fourier_Features` module (lines 160-193) calls `self.make_rng('params')` in its `setup()` method (line 179) to generate random weights for the Fourier feature matrix `B`. However, when the model is initialized (line 231) and later applied during training (line 270), **no RNG key is provided** for the 'params' collection.

**Why This Happens:**
Flax modules that use `self.make_rng()` require the caller to explicitly pass an `rngs` argument during `init()` and `apply()`. The current code does:
```python
params = model.init(args.key, dummy_t, dummy_x)['params']  # Line 231
```
But it should be:
```python
params = model.init({'params': args.key}, dummy_t, dummy_x)['params']
```

Similarly, all calls to `apply_fn()` (e.g., line 270) must include the RNG:
```python
u_ic_pred = apply_fn({'params': params, 'rngs': {'params': some_key}}, t_ic, x_ic)
```

**Fix Instructions for the Planner:**
1. **At Line 231 (Model Initialization):** Change `model.init(args.key, ...)` to `model.init({'params': args.key}, ...)`.
2. **At All `apply_fn()` Calls:** Add an `rngs` argument. For example, at line 270:
   ```python
   key, subkey = jax.random.split(key)  # Generate a fresh key
   u_ic_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_ic, x_ic, rngs={'params': subkey})
   ```
   Repeat this pattern for **every** `apply_fn()` call in the loss functions (lines 270, 274, 275, 297, 301, 302, 383, 427) and residual computation (line 256).

3. **Alternative (Simpler) Fix:** If the Fourier features are meant to be **static** (not re-randomized during inference), move the random matrix generation to `__init__` instead of `setup()`, and store it as a non-trainable parameter using `self.variable('params', 'B', ...)`. This avoids needing RNG keys during `apply()`.

*   **New Code (lines 3-25):**
    ```python
    def PDE_residuals(params, t, x, apply_fn, key):
        """
        Computes the residual of the KdV equation: f = u_t + 6*u*u_x + u_xxx
        """
        def u_scalar(t_s, x_s):
            # Note: Using the same key for all scalar calls in a vmap is acceptable
            # as the randomness is only used for feature generation, which is static here.
            return apply_fn({'params': params}, t_s.reshape(1, -1), x_s.reshape(1, -1), rngs={'params': key})[0, 0]

        u_t_fn = jax.grad(u_scalar, argnums=0)
        u_x_fn = jax.grad(u_scalar, argnums=1)
        u_xx_fn = jax.grad(u_x_fn, argnums=1)
        u_xxx_fn = jax.grad(u_xx_fn, argnums=1)

        u_t_vmap = vmap(u_t_fn)
        u_x_vmap = vmap(u_x_fn)
        u_xxx_vmap = vmap(u_xxx_fn)

        u_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t, x, rngs={'params': key})
        u_t = u_t_vmap(t.flatten(), x.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
        u_x = u_x_vmap(t.flatten(), x.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
        u_xxx = u_xxx_vmap(t.flatten(), x.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)

        residual = u_t + 6.0 * u_pred * u_x + u_xxx
        return residual
    ```
*   **Action:** Modify `loss_fn_phase1` to accept and use a PRNG key for all `apply_fn` calls.
*   **New Code (lines 29-50):**
    ```python
    @jit
    def loss_fn_phase1(params, key):
        """Loss for Phase 1: Data-driven (IC/BC only)."""
        # Split key for different data components to ensure independence if needed, good practice
        k1, k2, k3, k4 = jax.random.split(key, 4)

        # 1. Initial Condition Loss
        u_ic_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_ic, x_ic, rngs={'params': k1})
        loss_ic = jnp.mean((u_ic_pred - u_ic) ** 2)

        # 2. Boundary Condition Losses (Dirichlet)
        u_bc1_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_bc1, x_bc1, rngs={'params': k2})  # Left
        u_bc2_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_bc2, x_bc2, rngs={'params': k3})  # Right
        loss_bc = jnp.mean((u_bc1_pred - u_bc1) ** 2) + jnp.mean((u_bc2_pred - u_bc2) ** 2)

        # 3. Neumann Boundary Condition Loss
        def u_scalar_for_grad(t_s, x_s):
            return apply_fn({'params': params}, t_s.reshape(1, -1), x_s.reshape(1, -1), rngs={'params': k4})[0, 0]
        u_x_model_fn = vmap(jax.grad(u_scalar_for_grad, argnums=1))
        u_x_bc3_pred = u_x_model_fn(t_bc3.flatten(), x_bc3.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
        loss_neumann = jnp.mean((u_x_bc3_pred - u_x_bc3) ** 2)

        total_loss = loss_ic + loss_bc + loss_neumann
        loss_components = {"total": total_loss, "pde": 0.0, "ic": loss_ic, "bc": loss_bc, "neumann": loss_neumann}
        return total_loss, loss_components
    ```
*   **Action:** Modify `loss_fn_phase2` to accept and use a PRNG key for all `apply_fn` and `PDE_residuals` calls.
*   **New Code (lines 52-78):**
    ```python
    @jit
    def loss_fn_phase2(params, key):
        """Loss for Phase 2: Physics-informed (PDE + weighted data)."""
        # Split key for different loss components
        k_pde, k_ic, k_bc1, k_bc2, k_bc3 = jax.random.split(key, 5)

        # 1. PDE Loss
        f_pred = PDE_residuals(params, t_f, x_f, apply_fn, k_pde)
        loss_pde = jnp.mean(f_pred ** 2)

        # 2. Initial Condition Loss
        u_ic_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_ic, x_ic, rngs={'params': k_ic})
        loss_ic = jnp.mean((u_ic_pred - u_ic) ** 2)

        # 3. Boundary Condition Losses (Dirichlet)
        u_bc1_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_bc1, x_bc1, rngs={'params': k_bc1})  # Left
        u_bc2_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_bc2, x_bc2, rngs={'params': k_bc2})  # Right
        loss_bc = jnp.mean((u_bc1_pred - u_bc1) ** 2) + jnp.mean((u_bc2_pred - u_bc2) ** 2)

        # 4. Neumann Boundary Condition Loss
        def u_scalar_for_grad(t_s, x_s):
            return apply_fn({'params': params}, t_s.reshape(1, -1), x_s.reshape(1, -1), rngs={'params': k_bc3})[0, 0]
        u_x_model_fn = vmap(jax.grad(u_scalar_for_grad, argnums=1))
        u_x_bc3_pred = u_x_model_fn(t_bc3.flatten(), x_bc3.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
        loss_neumann = jnp.mean((u_x_bc3_pred - u_x_bc3) ** 2)

        loss_data = loss_ic + loss_bc + loss_neumann
        total_loss = args.lambda_pde * loss_pde + args.lambda_data * loss_data
        loss_components = {"total": total_loss, "pde": loss_pde, "ic": loss_ic, "bc": loss_bc, "neumann": loss_neumann}
        return total_loss, loss_components
    ```
*   **Action:** Update the JIT-compiled training steps to handle the new `key` argument in the loss functions.

    ```python
    @jit
    def train_step_phase1(params, opt_state, key):
        (loss_val, loss_components), grads = value_and_grad(loss_fn_phase1, argnums=0, has_aux=True)(params, key)
        updates, opt_state = optimizer_phase1.update(grads, opt_state, params)
    ```

Inspector summary:
 Fourier Feature MLP architecture with input embedding via Periodic Fourier Features (m=20, sigma=1.0) producing 40D features from normalized (t,x) inputs, followed by 4 hidden layers of 64 neurons each using tanh activation, and 1 output for u. Optimizer workflow uses Optax Adam with curriculum learning: Phase 1 (iterations 0-5000) optimizes only boundary/initial data losses (L_IC + L_BC_Left + L_BC_Right + L_Neumann) at constant LR=1e-3; Phase 2 (5001-50000) optimizes L_total = 1.0 * L_PDE + 100.0 * (L_IC + L_BC_Left + L_BC_Right + L_Neumann) with exponential decay LR schedule starting at 1e-3, decaying every 1000 steps by factor 0.9 towards approximately 1e-5. Specific loss terms: L_PDE = mean((u_t + 6*u*u_x + u_xxx)^2), L_IC/BC/Neumann as MSE on exact u or u_x targets. Reports relative L2 error on 256x100 validation grid every 500 iterations.
Proposed name:KdV_Mixed_AnalyticalIC\Question :
We are solving a forward problem for the Korteweg-De Vries (KdV) equation:

$$
u_t + 6uu_x + u_{xxx} = 0
$$

**Domain:**
- Spatial: $x \in [0, 20]$
- Temporal: $t \in [0, 5]$

**Boundary Conditions:**

$$
u(0,x) = g_0(x) \quad 	ext{(Initial Condition)}
$$

$$
u(t,0) = g_1(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=0	ext{)}
$$

$$
u(t,20) = g_2(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

$$
u_x(t,20) = g_3(t) \quad 	ext{(Neumann BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

**Analytical Solution:**

The solution is given by a two-soliton interaction formula:

$$
ight]^2}osh\left(rac{\sqrt{c_1}\zeta_1 - \sqrt{c_2}\zeta_2}{2}_2}\zeta_2}{2}
$$


where:
- $\zeta_1 = x - c_1 t - x_1$
- $\zeta_2 = x - c_2 t - x_2$

**Parameters:**
- $c_1 = 6.0$ (velocity of first soliton)
- $c_2 = 2.0$ (velocity of second soliton)
- $x_1 = -2.0$ (initial position of first soliton)
- $x_2 = 2.0$ (initial position of second soliton)

The boundary conditions $g_0, g_1, g_2, g_3$ are derived by evaluating the analytical solution at the appropriate boundaries.

CONSTRAINTS: If using latin hypercube sampling dont use any criterion such as 'maximin'.

**Section 3: Forensic Analysis & Feedback**
*   **Observation:**
    1.  **Loss & Error History:** The loss plot clearly shows the two-phase curriculum learning. In Phase 1 (0-5k iterations), the boundary/initial condition loss successfully minimizes to a low value (~1.0). At iteration 5001, when the PDE loss is introduced, the total loss jumps to ~100 and remains completely flat for the rest of the training. The Relative L2 Error drops to ~90% and also flatlines, showing no learning is occurring in Phase 2.
    2.  **Solution Fields:** The "Prediction" plot is uniformly blue, corresponding to a value of zero. The "Pointwise Error" plot is a mirror image of the "Reference" solution, confirming that the model has predicted the trivial solution, $u(x,t) = 0$, across the entire domain.
    3.  **PDE Residual:** The PDE residual plot shows values between 1e-3 and 1e-8. This is consistent with the trivial solution, as $u=0$ makes the PDE equation $0_t + 6(0)u_x + 0_{xxx} = 0$, which is perfectly satisfied.

*   **Diagnosis:** **Trivial Solution Collapse due to Insufficient Boundary Enforcement.**
    The model has converged to the trivial solution, $u(x,t)=0$. This is the exact same failure mode as the previous attempt, indicating that the implemented curriculum learning strategy was ineffective. While the model learned the boundaries in Phase 1, the moment the PDE loss was introduced in Phase 2, the optimizer found a powerful local minimum by setting $u=0$. This perfectly satisfies the PDE term ($f=0$), and the resulting penalty from the boundary conditions (even with a weight of $\lambda=100$) was insufficient to pull the solution out of this basin of attraction. The optimization completely stalled.

*   **Targeted Improvements:**
    *   **For Integrity:** The run was stable and completed without errors. No improvements are needed here.
    *   **For Accuracy:** The boundary condition enforcement must be significantly stronger.
        *   **Increase Boundary Weight:** The weight $\lambda=100$ was clearly not enough. Increase it substantially, perhaps to `1000` or even `10000`, to make violating the boundary conditions far more costly than a non-zero PDE residual.
        *   **Loss Annealing:** Instead of a hard switch from Phase 1 to Phase 2, consider a gradual introduction of the PDE loss. For example, from iterations 5k to 15k, gradually increase the PDE loss weight from 0 to 1. This "annealing" approach can prevent the optimizer from making a sudden jump to the trivial solution.
    *   **For Details:** Not applicable. The model must first learn the basic solution structure before fine-grained details can be assessed.
    *   **For Optimality:** The current strategy is suboptimal as it leads to immediate stagnation. The suggestions for accuracy (increasing $\lambda$, loss annealing) are the primary ways to improve the methodology. Additionally, consider a lower initial learning rate for Phase 2 to encourage smaller, more stable steps away from the boundary-fitted solution.

--- Strategy Report Received ---
To the Planner:

The previous attempts failed due to a "trivial solution collapse" ($u=0$), where the optimizer minimized the PDE residual (which is 0 for $u=0$) at the expense of the boundary conditions, despite curriculum learning. To fix this, we will implement a **Loss Annealing** strategy with **significantly higher boundary weights** and a robust MLP architecture.

Here is the scientific plan:

### 1. Data Source
**Generate Synthetic Data:**
You must implement the analytical solution in JAX to generate the training and validation data.
*   **Formula:**
    $$u(x,t) = \frac{2(c_1 - c_2) \left[ c_1 \cosh^2\left(\frac{\sqrt{c_2}\zeta_2}{2}\right) + c_2 \sinh^2\left(\frac{\sqrt{c_1}\zeta_1}{2}\right) \right]}{\left[ \left(\sqrt{c_1} - \sqrt{c_2}\right) \cosh\left(\frac{\sqrt{c_1}\zeta_1 + \sqrt{c_2}\zeta_2}{2}\right) + \left(\sqrt{c_1} + \sqrt{c_2}\right) \cosh\left(\frac{\sqrt{c_1}\zeta_1 - \sqrt{c_2}\zeta_2}{2}\right) \right]^2}$$
    where:
    *   $\zeta_1 = x - c_1 t - x_1$
    *   $\zeta_2 = x - c_2 t - x_2$
    *   $c_1 = 6.0, c_2 = 2.0, x_1 = -2.0, x_2 = 2.0$
*   **Training Data:**
    *   **Collocation Points ($N_f=10000$):** Sample $t \in [0, 5], x \in [0, 20]$ using Latin Hypercube Sampling (or uniform random).
    *   **IC Points ($N_{ic}=200$):** $t=0, x \in [0, 20]$.
    *   **BC Points ($N_{bc}=200$ each):**
        *   Left: $x=0, t \in [0, 5]$
        *   Right: $x=20, t \in [0, 5]$
*   **Validation Data:**
    *   A grid of $256 \times 100$ points ($x \times t$) covering the domain.

### 2. Problem Statement
*   **Governing Equation (KdV):**
    $$u_t + 6uu_x + u_{xxx} = 0$$
    *   **Residual:** $r(u, x, t) = \frac{\partial u}{\partial t} + 6u\frac{\partial u}{\partial x} + \frac{\partial^3 u}{\partial x^3}$
*   **Boundary Conditions:**
    *   **IC:** $u(0,x) = g_0(x)$ (Dirichlet)
    *   **BC Left:** $u(t,0) = g_1(t)$ (Dirichlet)
    *   **BC Right:** $u(t,20) = g_2(t)$ (Dirichlet)
    *   **BC Right (Neumann):** $u_x(t,20) = g_3(t)$ (Neumann)
    *   *Note:* $g_0, g_1, g_2, g_3$ are the values of the analytical solution (or its derivative) at those coordinates.

### 3. Model Architecture
**Main Model:** Standard MLP with Input Normalization.
*   **Inputs:** $(t, x)$.
*   **Normalization:** **CRITICAL.** Normalize inputs to $[-1, 1]$ before passing to the layers.
    *   $t_{norm} = 2 \cdot \frac{t - 0}{5 - 0} - 1$
    *   $x_{norm} = 2 \cdot \frac{x - 0}{20 - 0} - 1$
*   **Layers:** 5 hidden layers with **128 neurons** each.
*   **Activation:** `tanh`.
*   **Output:** 1 neuron ($u$).
*   **Initialization:** Xavier/Glorot normal.

### 4. Optimization & Loss Function
**Optimization:**
*   **Optimizer:** `optax.adam`
*   **Gradient Clipping:** Apply `optax.clip_by_global_norm(1.0)` to prevent exploding gradients from the third derivative.
*   **Learning Rate:** Start at `1e-3`. Use a cosine decay schedule to `1e-5` over 50,000 iterations.
*   **Iterations:** 50,000.

**Loss Function (Annealing Strategy):**
To prevent trivial solution collapse, we will weight the BCs heavily and introduce the PDE loss gradually.
$$L_{total} = \lambda_{BC} (L_{IC} + L_{BC_{left}} + L_{BC_{right}} + L_{Neumann}) + \lambda_{PDE}(i) L_{PDE}$$

*   **Weights:**
    *   $\lambda_{BC} = 1000.0$ (Constant, high weight to enforce boundaries).
    *   $\lambda_{PDE}(i)$:
        *   **Phase 1 (Iter 0 - 5,000):** $\lambda_{PDE} = 0.0$ (Learn BCs only).
        *   **Phase 2 (Iter 5,000 - 15,000):** Linearly ramp $\lambda_{PDE}$ from $0.0$ to $1.0$.
        *   **Phase 3 (Iter 15,000 - 50,000):** $\lambda_{PDE} = 1.0$.

**Enhancements:**
*   **JAX Native:** Ensure all derivatives ($u_t, u_x, u_{xxx}$) are computed using `jax.grad` or `jax.jacfwd`.
*   **Reporting:** Calculate and print the Relative L2 Error against the validation set every 500 iterations.
*   **Neumann BC:** Compute $\frac{\partial u}{\partial x}$ at the boundary within the loss function using JAX AD.

**Output Section:**
**Main Model:** MLP (5x128, tanh) with Input Normalization and Adam Optimizer.
**Enhancements:** Loss Annealing (PDE weight ramp), Gradient Clipping, High BC weights.

    ```python
    class Args:
    ```
