--- Analizing: # ## Cell 4: Model ---
  - [Planner]: Instructions received:
### [CURRENT_CELL_ID]
*   **Action:** The `FourierFeaturePINN` and its helper class `Periodic_Fourier_Features` are being replaced with a standard `MLP` model as required by the scientific strategy. The new `MLP` class incorporates the mandatory input normalization layer. The model instantiation and parameter initialization logic are updated for the new, deterministic architecture.
*   **New Code:**
    ```python
    class MLP(nn.Module):
        """A standard Multi-Layer Perceptron."""
        features: Sequence[int]
        activation: Callable = nn.tanh
        t_domain: Sequence[float]
        x_domain: Sequence[float]
    
        @nn.compact
        def __call__(self, t, x):
            # --- Input Normalization ---
            # Normalize t and x to the range [-1, 1]
            t_min, t_max = self.t_domain
            x_min, x_max = self.x_domain
            t_norm = 2.0 * (t - t_min) / (t_max - t_min) - 1.0
            x_norm = 2.0 * (x - x_min) / (x_max - x_min) - 1.0
            X = jnp.hstack([t_norm, x_norm])
    
            # --- MLP Layers ---
            # The Glorot normal initializer is specified by the strategy.
            initializer = nn.initializers.glorot_normal()
    
            for i, units in enumerate(self.features[:-1]):
                X = nn.Dense(features=units, kernel_init=initializer)(X)
                X = self.activation(X)
            
            # Final layer (no activation)
            X = nn.Dense(features=self.features[-1], kernel_init=initializer)(X)
            return X
    
    # Instantiate the model
    # The first element of args.layers (input dim) is handled implicitly,
    # so we pass the subsequent layer dimensions.
    model = MLP(
        features=args.layers[1:],
        activation=args.activation,
        t_domain=args.t_domain,
        x_domain=args.x_domain
    )
    
    # Initialize parameters
    # Create a dummy input with the correct shape (N, 1) for t and x
    dummy_t = jnp.ones((1, 1))
    dummy_x = jnp.ones((1, 1))
    # The new model is deterministic and doesn't require a 'params' RNG stream for feature generation.
    # The key is passed directly for weight initialization.
    params = model.init(args.key, dummy_t, dummy_x)['params']
    
    # Define the model's apply function for predictions
    apply_fn = jit(model.apply)
    
    print(f"Model initialized with {sum(p.size for p in jax.tree_util.tree_leaves(params))} parameters.")
    ```

--- Analizing: # ## Cell 5: Optimizer Setup ---
  - [Planner]: Instructions received:
### [CURRENT_CELL_ID]
*   **Action:** This cell is being completely overhauled to implement the new loss annealing strategy. The previous two-phase loss functions, optimizers, and training steps are replaced with a single, unified system.
    1.  The `PDE_residuals` function is updated to be deterministic, removing the no-longer-needed `key` argument.
    2.  A new `loss_fn` is created. It takes the current iteration `it` as an argument to calculate the annealed `lambda_pde` weight on-the-fly, implementing the three-phase schedule (zero, ramp, one).
    3.  A single `optax` optimizer is defined, chaining the required `clip_by_global_norm` with `adam` and using a `cosine_decay_schedule` for the learning rate.
    4.  A single JIT-compiled `train_step` function is created to work with the new loss function and optimizer.
*   **New Code:**
    ```python
    # --- PDE Residual Function (Updated for deterministic model) ---
    def PDE_residuals(params, t, x, apply_fn):
        """
        Computes the residual of the KdV equation: f = u_t + 6*u*u_x + u_xxx
        """
        def u_scalar(t_s, x_s):
            # The model is now deterministic, no RNG key needed for apply_fn
            return apply_fn({'params': params}, t_s.reshape(1, -1), x_s.reshape(1, -1))[0, 0]
    
        # Higher-order derivatives via repeated jax.grad
        u_t_fn = jax.grad(u_scalar, argnums=0)
        u_x_fn = jax.grad(u_scalar, argnums=1)
        u_xx_fn = jax.grad(u_x_fn, argnums=1)
        u_xxx_fn = jax.grad(u_xx_fn, argnums=1)
    
        # Vectorize the derivative functions to apply them over the entire batch
        u_t_vmap = vmap(u_t_fn)
        u_x_vmap = vmap(u_x_fn)
        u_xxx_vmap = vmap(u_xxx_fn)
    
        # Compute model predictions and derivatives
        u_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t, x)
        u_t = u_t_vmap(t.flatten(), x.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
        u_x = u_x_vmap(t.flatten(), x.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
        u_xxx = u_xxx_vmap(t.flatten(), x.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
    
        residual = u_t + 6.0 * u_pred * u_x + u_xxx
        return residual
    
    # --- Unified Loss Function with Annealing ---
    @jit
    def loss_fn(params, it):
        """Unified loss function with annealed PDE weight."""
        
        # 1. PDE Loss
        f_pred = PDE_residuals(params, t_f, x_f, apply_fn)
        loss_pde = jnp.mean(f_pred ** 2)
    
        # 2. Initial Condition Loss
        u_ic_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_ic, x_ic)
        loss_ic = jnp.mean((u_ic_pred - u_ic) ** 2)
    
        # 3. Boundary Condition Losses (Dirichlet)
        u_bc1_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_bc1, x_bc1)  # Left
        u_bc2_pred = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_bc2, x_bc2)  # Right
        loss_bc_dirichlet = jnp.mean((u_bc1_pred - u_bc1) ** 2) + jnp.mean((u_bc2_pred - u_bc2) ** 2)
    
        # 4. Neumann Boundary Condition Loss
        def u_scalar_for_grad(t_s, x_s):
            return apply_fn({'params': params}, t_s.reshape(1, -1), x_s.reshape(1, -1))[0, 0]
        u_x_model_fn = vmap(jax.grad(u_scalar_for_grad, argnums=1))
        u_x_bc3_pred = u_x_model_fn(t_bc3.flatten(), x_bc3.flatten()).reshape(-1, 1)
        loss_neumann = jnp.mean((u_x_bc3_pred - u_x_bc3) ** 2)
    
        # Combine all data-driven losses
        loss_data = loss_ic + loss_bc_dirichlet + loss_neumann
    
        # 5. Annealing logic for PDE loss weight
        progress = jnp.clip((it - args.anneal_start) / (args.anneal_end - args.anneal_start), 0.0, 1.0)
        lambda_pde_annealed = lax.cond(it < args.anneal_start, lambda: 0.0, lambda: progress)
    
        # 6. Total Loss Calculation
        total_loss = args.lambda_bc * loss_data + lambda_pde_annealed * loss_pde
        
        loss_components = {
            "total": total_loss, "pde": loss_pde, "ic": loss_ic, 
            "bc": loss_bc_dirichlet, "neumann": loss_neumann, "lambda_pde": lambda_pde_annealed
        }
        return total_loss, loss_components
    
    # --- Optimizer Setup with Cosine Decay and Gradient Clipping ---
    lr_schedule = optax.cosine_decay_schedule(
        init_value=args.learning_rate_start,
        decay_steps=args.iterations,
        alpha=args.learning_rate_end / args.learning_rate_start
    )
    
    optimizer = optax.chain(
        optax.clip_by_global_norm(args.clip_norm),
        optax.adam(learning_rate=lr_schedule)
    )
    
    opt_state = optimizer.init(params)
    
    # --- JIT-Compiled Unified Training Step ---
    @jit
    def train_step(params, opt_state, it):
        (loss_val, loss_components), grads = value_and_grad(loss_fn, argnums=0, has_aux=True)(params, it)
        updates, opt_state = optimizer.update(grads, opt_state, params)
        params = optax.apply_updates(params, updates)
        return params, opt_state, loss_val, loss_components
    
    print("Optimizer with annealing and unified training step initialized.")
    ```

### [CURRENT_CELL_ID]
*   **Action:** The training loop is being refactored to use the new unified `train_step` and loss annealing strategy. The complex `if/else` logic for curriculum learning is removed. The loop now calls the single `train_step`, passing the current iteration `it`. The prediction call for validation is updated to remove the obsolete RNG key, and the progress bar is updated to display the new annealed PDE loss weight (`lambda_pde`).
*   **New Code:**
    ```python
    # --- Main Training Loop ---
    print("Starting training with loss annealing...")
    start_time = time.time()
    
    # Lists to store metrics
    all_its = []
    all_loss = []
    all_errors = []
    all_L_2 = []
    all_L_infty = []
    
    # Use tqdm for a progress bar
    pbar = tqdm(range(args.iterations), desc="Training")
    
    for it in pbar:
        # --- Unified Training Step ---
        # The train_step now takes the iteration number to handle annealing internally
        params, opt_state, loss_val, loss_components = train_step(params, opt_state, it)
    
        # --- Reporting and Error Calculation ---
        if it % args.report_every == 0 or it == args.iterations - 1:
            # Predict solution on the validation grid (no RNG key needed)
            u_pred_val = apply_fn({'params': params}, t_val.reshape(-1, 1), x_val.reshape(-1, 1))
            u_pred_val = u_pred_val.reshape(Exact.shape)
            
            # Calculate relative L2 error
            error_l2 = jnp.linalg.norm(u_pred_val - Exact) / jnp.linalg.norm(Exact)
            # Calculate L-infinity error
            error_linf = jnp.max(jnp.abs(u_pred_val - Exact))
            
            # Store metrics
            all_its.append(it)
            all_loss.append(loss_val.item())
            all_errors.append(error_l2.item())
            all_L_2.append(jnp.linalg.norm(u_pred_val - Exact).item())
            all_L_infty.append(error_linf.item())
            
            # Update progress bar description
            pbar.set_postfix({
                "Loss": f"{loss_val.item():.4e}",
                "L2 Error": f"{error_l2.item():.4e}",
                "PDE": f"{loss_components['pde']:.2e}",
                "IC": f"{loss_components['ic']:.2e}",
                "BC": f"{loss_components['bc']:.2e}",
                "Neumann": f"{loss_components['neumann']:.2e}",
                "λ_pde": f"{loss_components['lambda_pde']:.2f}"
            })
    
    # --- Finalization ---
    training_time = time.time() - start_time
    print(f"\nTraining finished in {training_time:.2f} seconds.")
    
    # Store the final trained parameters for evaluation and plotting
    current_params_for_eval = params
    ```

Proposed name:KdV_Mixed_AnalyticalIC\Question :
We are solving a forward problem for the Korteweg-De Vries (KdV) equation:

$$
u_t + 6uu_x + u_{xxx} = 0
$$

**Domain:**
- Spatial: $x \in [0, 20]$
- Temporal: $t \in [0, 5]$

**Boundary Conditions:**

$$
u(0,x) = g_0(x) \quad 	ext{(Initial Condition)}
$$

$$
u(t,0) = g_1(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=0	ext{)}
$$

$$
u(t,20) = g_2(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

$$
u_x(t,20) = g_3(t) \quad 	ext{(Neumann BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

**Analytical Solution:**

The solution is given by a two-soliton interaction formula:

$$
ight]^2}osh\left(rac{\sqrt{c_1}\zeta_1 - \sqrt{c_2}\zeta_2}{2}_2}\zeta_2}{2}
$$


where:
- $\zeta_1 = x - c_1 t - x_1$
- $\zeta_2 = x - c_2 t - x_2$

**Parameters:**
- $c_1 = 6.0$ (velocity of first soliton)
- $c_2 = 2.0$ (velocity of second soliton)
- $x_1 = -2.0$ (initial position of first soliton)
- $x_2 = 2.0$ (initial position of second soliton)

The boundary conditions $g_0, g_1, g_2, g_3$ are derived by evaluating the analytical solution at the appropriate boundaries.

CONSTRAINTS: If using latin hypercube sampling dont use any criterion such as 'maximin'.

--- Running Debugging Team ---
Debugger Status: REJECTED_FATAL
Debugger Analysis: **Cell 4, Line 177: TypeError in Model Initialization**

The error occurred during model parameter initialization at line 196:
```python
params = model.init(args.key, dummy_t, dummy_x)['params']
```

The traceback shows the failure happens inside the `MLP.__call__` method at line 177:
```python
X = self.activation(X)
```

The error message is:
```
TypeError: Error interpreting argument to <function tanh at 0x7fa23d4d3920> as an abstract array. The problematic value is of type <class '__main__.Args'> and was passed to the function at path args[0].
```

**Root Cause Analysis:**

This is a **Flax module configuration error**. The issue is that `self.activation` is being set to `args.activation` (line 186), which is `nn.tanh` (line 57). However, during the `@nn.compact` method execution, Flax is trying to call `self.activation(X)`, but `self.activation` is being interpreted as the `Args` class instance instead of the activation function.

**The Bug:**

In line 162, the model defines:
```python
activation: Callable = nn.tanh
```

But in line 186, the model is instantiated with:
```python
activation=args.activation,  # This is nn.tanh from line 57
```

The problem is that Flax's `@nn.compact` decorator is misinterpreting the `activation` attribute. When you pass `nn.tanh` as a module attribute in Flax, it needs to be handled carefully. The error suggests that during JIT compilation, JAX is seeing the `Args` class instead of the function.

**The Fix:**

The Planner must change line 162 to store the activation function correctly. The standard pattern in Flax is to either:

1. **Option A (Recommended):** Store the activation as a static field by not making it a dataclass field:
```python
class MLP(nn.Module):
    features: Sequence[int]
    t_domain: Sequence[float]
    x_domain: Sequence[float]
    
    @nn.compact
    def __call__(self, t, x):
        activation = nn.tanh  # Define it locally
        # ... rest of code
        X = activation(X)
```

2. **Option B:** Pass it as a callable that's not a Flax module:
```python
activation: Callable = jax.nn.tanh  # Use jax.nn.tanh instead of nn.tanh
```

The issue is that `nn.tanh` is a Flax module, not a pure function, and passing it as a field causes initialization issues during `model.init()`.

**Recommended Fix:**
Replace line 162 with:
```python
# Remove activation from class fields
```

And modify line 177 to:
```python
X = nn.tanh(X)  # Call nn.tanh directly
```

This avoids the field assignment issue entirely and uses the activation function directly in the forward pass.

Proposed name:KdV_Mixed_AnalyticalIC\Question :
We are solving a forward problem for the Korteweg-De Vries (KdV) equation:

$$
u_t + 6uu_x + u_{xxx} = 0
$$

**Domain:**
- Spatial: $x \in [0, 20]$
- Temporal: $t \in [0, 5]$

**Boundary Conditions:**

$$
u(0,x) = g_0(x) \quad 	ext{(Initial Condition)}
$$

$$
u(t,0) = g_1(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=0	ext{)}
$$

$$
u(t,20) = g_2(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

$$
u_x(t,20) = g_3(t) \quad 	ext{(Neumann BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

**Analytical Solution:**

The solution is given by a two-soliton interaction formula:

$$
ight]^2}osh\left(rac{\sqrt{c_1}\zeta_1 - \sqrt{c_2}\zeta_2}{2}_2}\zeta_2}{2}
$$


where:
- $\zeta_1 = x - c_1 t - x_1$
- $\zeta_2 = x - c_2 t - x_2$

**Parameters:**
- $c_1 = 6.0$ (velocity of first soliton)
- $c_2 = 2.0$ (velocity of second soliton)
- $x_1 = -2.0$ (initial position of first soliton)
- $x_2 = 2.0$ (initial position of second soliton)

The boundary conditions $g_0, g_1, g_2, g_3$ are derived by evaluating the analytical solution at the appropriate boundaries.

**Section 3: Forensic Analysis & Feedback**
*   **Observation:**
    1.  **Training Stability:** The training run was perfectly stable, completing all 50,000 iterations without exploding gradients. The loss annealing strategy is clearly visible in the loss history plot, with a sharp increase in loss at iteration 5,000 when the PDE term was introduced. This confirms the methodology was implemented correctly and successfully prevented the "trivial solution collapse" seen in prior attempts.
    2.  **Solution Accuracy:** Despite the stable training, the final solution is physically incorrect. The "Prediction" plot shows that the model has only learned the faster, larger soliton. The second, slower soliton is completely absent from the solution.
    3.  **Error Analysis:** This failure is quantified by the very high final Relative L2 Error of **52.4%**, as confirmed by both the plot and the STDOUT log. The "Pointwise Error" plot pinpoints the source of this error, showing a massive discrepancy (red region, error of order 1e0) precisely along the trajectory where the second soliton should be.
    4.  **PDE Residual:** The residual plot shows high values along the path of the *predicted* soliton and, interestingly, also along the path of the *missing* soliton. This indicates the network is struggling to satisfy the PDE in the physically active regions of the domain.

*   **Diagnosis:** The primary failure mode is **Mode Collapse** (a form of severe underfitting). The model found a simple, but incorrect, local minimum in the loss landscape. It learned to represent the single most dominant feature of the solution (the large soliton), which partially satisfies the PDE and the boundary conditions, but it lacked the capacity or the optimization pressure to form the second, more subtle feature. The high-order derivatives and strong non-linearity of the KdV equation create a complex optimization challenge that the standard MLP architecture could not overcome.

*   **Targeted Improvements:**
    *   **For Integrity:** The integrity was excellent. No changes are needed here. The loss annealing and gradient clipping strategy should be maintained as it provides a stable training baseline.
    *   **For Accuracy:**
        1.  **Increase Model Capacity:** The 5x128 MLP may be too small to represent the complex two-soliton interaction. A deeper and wider network (e.g., 8 layers x 256 neurons) should be tested to see if capacity is the bottleneck.
        2.  **Improve Architectural Bias:** A standard MLP may struggle with the high-frequency components of the solution. Consider replacing the MLP with a **Fourier Feature Network (FFN)** or a **SIREN** (Sinusoidal Representation Network) architecture, which are known to be better at learning complex functions.
    *   **For Details:** The immediate goal is to capture the second soliton. Finer details can be addressed after this primary failure is resolved.
    *   **For Optimality:**
        1.  **Re-evaluate Loss Weights:** The constant, high boundary weight ($\lambda_{BC}=1000$) might be overly restrictive, preventing the PDE loss from properly shaping the solution in the interior. Experiment with a lower, but still significant, weight (e.g., 100).
        2.  **Refine Sampling Strategy:** The current uniform sampling might not place enough emphasis on the interaction region. Consider implementing a form of **Residual-based Adaptive Refinement (RAR)**, where new collocation points are periodically added in regions of high PDE residual to focus the network's attention.

To the Planner:

The previous attempt (Run 16) achieved stable training but suffered from **Mode Collapse**, capturing only the dominant soliton and missing the second one, resulting in a 52% error. Here is the scientific plan to solve the KdV equation.

### 1. Data Source & Problem Statement
**Governing Equation:**
The Korteweg-De Vries equation:
$$u_t + 6uu_x + u_{xxx} = 0$$
*   **Residual:** $f = \frac{\partial u}{\partial t} + 6u\frac{\partial u}{\partial x} + \frac{\partial^3 u}{\partial x^3}$

**Domain:**
$t \in [0, 5]$, $x \in [0, 20]$.
*   **Normalization:** You **MUST** normalize inputs $(t, x)$ to the range $[-1, 1]$ before passing them into the network layers.

**Boundary/Initial Conditions (Exact Data Generation):**
You must implement the analytical solution function `u_exact(t, x)` exactly as provided in the prompt to generate the training data for boundaries and initial conditions ($g_0, g_1, g_2, g_3$).
*   **IC:** $u(0, x)$ for $x \in [0, 20]$
*   **BC Left:** $u(t, 0)$ for $t \in [0, 5]$
*   **BC Right:** $u(t, 20)$ for $t \in [0, 5]$
*   **Neumann BC Right:** $u_x(t, 20)$ for $t \in [0, 5]$

### 2. Model Architecture
**Main Model:**
*   **Type:** Deep MLP (Multi-Layer Perceptron).
*   **Depth/Width:** **8 hidden layers** with **128 neurons** each (Increased capacity as per Advisor).
*   **Activation:** `tanh` (Hyperbolic Tangent).
*   **Output:** 1 neuron ($u$).
*   **Initialization:** Xavier/Glorot normal.

### 3. Optimization Strategy
*   **Optimizer:** `optax.adam` (JAX native).
*   **Learning Rate:** Cosine Decay Schedule.
    *   Start: `1e-3`
    *   End: `1e-5`
    *   Steps: 50,000
*   **Gradient Clipping:** Apply global gradient clipping with max norm `1.0` to maintain stability.

### 4. Training & Loss Formulation
**Loss Function:**
$$ \mathcal{L}_{total} = \lambda_{PDE}(i) \mathcal{L}_{PDE} + \lambda_{BC} (\mathcal{L}_{IC} + \mathcal{L}_{BC\_left} + \mathcal{L}_{BC\_right} + \mathcal{L}_{Neumann}) $$

**Weights:**
*   $\lambda_{BC} = 100.0$ (Reduced from 1000 to allow PDE physics to influence the interior more).
*   $\lambda_{PDE}(i)$: **Annealing Schedule**
    *   Iterations 0 - 5,000: $\lambda_{PDE} = 0.0$ (Pre-training on BCs/ICs).
    *   Iterations 5,000 - 10,000: Linearly ramp from $0.0 \to 1.0$.
    *   Iterations 10,000+: $\lambda_{PDE} = 1.0$.

**Enhancements (CRITICAL):**
1.  **Residual-based Adaptive Refinement (RAR):**
    *   **Initial Collocation:** 2,000 points (Latin Hypercube Sampling, `criterion=None`).
    *   **Refinement Loop:** Every **2,500 iterations** (starting after iter 10,000):
        1.  Generate a dense candidate set of random points (e.g., 10,000) in the domain.
        2.  Compute the PDE residual $|f|$ for all candidate points.
        3.  Select the **top 200 points** with the highest residual.
        4.  Add these 200 points to the training collocation set for subsequent iterations.
    *   This strategy explicitly targets the "missing soliton" region where errors are high.

### 5. Execution Plan
1.  **Define `u_exact`**: Implement the two-soliton formula carefully.
2.  **Data Prep**: Generate fixed BC/IC points (200 each). Generate initial Collocation points (2000).
3.  **Training Loop (50,000 steps)**:
    *   Update Learning Rate.
    *   Update $\lambda_{PDE}$.
    *   **If** `step % 2500 == 0` and `step >= 10000`: Perform RAR (evaluate residuals, add points).
    *   Compute Loss and Gradients.
    *   Update Parameters.
    *   **Every 500 steps**: Report Loss terms and Relative L2 Error against the analytical solution on a validation grid ($256_t \times 100_x$).

**Output Section:**
**Main Model:** Deep MLP (8 layers, 128 units, tanh), Optax Adam with Cosine Decay.
**Enhancements:** Residual-based Adaptive Refinement (RAR), Loss Annealing (PDE weight ramp-up), Gradient Clipping.

*   **Action:** Implement the Residual-based Adaptive Refinement (RAR) logic inside the main training loop. This involves generating candidate points, calculating their PDE residuals, and adding the points with the highest residuals to the training set at specified intervals. *   **New Code (lines 14-18):**
    ```python
    # The main key needs to be accessible and updatable inside the loop for RAR
    key = args.key

    for it in pbar:
        # --- Residual-based Adaptive Refinement (RAR) ---
        if it % args.rar_every == 0 and it >= args.rar_start:
            # Generate candidate points for refinement
            key, subkey = jax.random.split(key)
            t_cand = jax.random.uniform(subkey, (args.rar_candidates, 1), minval=args.t_domain[0], maxval=args.t_domain[1])
            key, subkey = jax.random.split(key)
            x_cand = jax.random.uniform(subkey, (args.rar_candidates, 1), minval=args.x_domain[0], maxval=args.x_domain[1])

            # Compute residuals on the candidate points
            f_cand = PDE_residuals(params, t_cand, x_cand, apply_fn)
            f_cand_abs = jnp.abs(f_cand).flatten()

            # Find the indices of the points with the highest residuals
            top_indices = jnp.argsort(f_cand_abs)[-args.rar_add:]

            # Add these high-residual points to the training set
            t_f = jnp.vstack([t_f, t_cand[top_indices]])
            x_f = jnp.vstack([x_f, x_cand[top_indices]])
            
            # Update description to show new number of points
            pbar.set_description(f"Training (N_f={t_f.shape[0]})")

        # --- Unified Training Step ---
        # Pass the (potentially updated) t_f and x_f to the train_step
        params, opt_state, loss_val, loss_components = train_step(params, opt_state, it, t_f, x_f)
    ```

The loss function assembles L_total = λ_PDE(i) * L_PDE + λ_BC * (L_IC + L_BC_left + L_BC_right + L_Neumann), where λ_BC=100.0 is fixed and λ_PDE follows the exact annealing schedule: 0.0 for iterations 0-5000, linear ramp from 0.0 to 1.0 during 5000-10000, and 1.0 thereafter. Proposed name:KdV_Mixed_AnalyticalIC\Question :
We are solving a forward problem for the Korteweg-De Vries (KdV) equation:

$$
u_t + 6uu_x + u_{xxx} = 0
$$

**Domain:**
- Spatial: $x \in [0, 20]$
- Temporal: $t \in [0, 5]$

**Boundary Conditions:**

$$
u(0,x) = g_0(x) \quad 	ext{(Initial Condition)}
$$

$$
u(t,0) = g_1(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=0	ext{)}
$$

$$
u(t,20) = g_2(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

$$
u_x(t,20) = g_3(t) \quad 	ext{(Neumann BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

**Analytical Solution:**

The solution is given by a two-soliton interaction formula:

$$
ight]^2}osh\left(rac{\sqrt{c_1}\zeta_1 - \sqrt{c_2}\zeta_2}{2}_2}\zeta_2}{2}
$$


where:
- $\zeta_1 = x - c_1 t - x_1$
- $\zeta_2 = x - c_2 t - x_2$

**Parameters:**
- $c_1 = 6.0$ (velocity of first soliton)
- $c_2 = 2.0$ (velocity of second soliton)
- $x_1 = -2.0$ (initial position of first soliton)
- $x_2 = 2.0$ (initial position of second soliton)

The boundary conditions $g_0, g_1, g_2, g_3$ are derived by evaluating the analytical solution at the appropriate boundaries.

MANDATORY: Make sure to report the loss and errors every 500 iterations and you cannonly use optimizers that work natively in JAX (NO SciPY)
CONSTRAINTS: If using latin hypercube sampling dont use any criterion such as 'maximin'.

The log shows an `IndentationError` at Line 276 in the `train_step` function definition. The error message states: 'expected an indented block after function definition on line 275'.

**Root Cause:**
Line 275 defines the function signature for `train_step`:
```python
def train_step(params, opt_state, it, t_f, x_f):
```
However, Line 276 (the first line of the function body) is **not indented**:
```python
(loss_val, loss_components), grads = value_and_grad(loss_fn, argnums=0, has_aux=True)(params, it, t_f, x_f)
```

In Python, all code inside a function must be indented (typically 4 spaces). This line is currently at the same indentation level as the `def` statement, which is a syntax error.

**Fix Required:**

```python
@jit
def train_step(params, opt_state, it, t_f, x_f):
    (loss_val, loss_components), grads = value_and_grad(loss_fn, argnums=0, has_aux=True)(params, it, t_f, x_f)
    updates, opt_state = optimizer.update(grads, opt_state, params)
    params = optax.apply_updates(params, updates)
    return params, opt_state, loss_val, loss_components
```

The loss function assembles L_total = λ_PDE(i) * L_PDE + λ_BC * (L_IC + L_BC_left + L_BC_right + L_Neumann), where λ_BC=100.0 is fixed and λ_PDE follows the exact annealing schedule: 0.0 for iterations 0-5000, linear ramp from 0.0 to 1.0 during 5000-10000, and 1.0 thereafter. Training includes Residual-based Adaptive Refinement (RAR): starting after iteration 10000, every 2500 steps, evaluate PDE residuals on 10,000 uniform random candidate points in the domain and append the top 200 highest-residual points to the collocation set (initially 2000 LHS points). Additional BC/IC points are fixed at 200 each. Reporting occurs every 500 steps with relative L2 error on a 256 x 100 validation grid against the analytical solution.
Proposed name:KdV_Mixed_AnalyticalIC\Question :
We are solving a forward problem for the Korteweg-De Vries (KdV) equation:

$$
u_t + 6uu_x + u_{xxx} = 0
$$

**Domain:**
- Spatial: $x \in [0, 20]$
- Temporal: $t \in [0, 5]$

**Boundary Conditions:**

$$
u(0,x) = g_0(x) \quad 	ext{(Initial Condition)}
$$

$$
u(t,0) = g_1(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=0	ext{)}
$$

$$
u(t,20) = g_2(t) \quad 	ext{(BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

$$
u_x(t,20) = g_3(t) \quad 	ext{(Neumann BC at } x=20	ext{)}
$$

**Analytical Solution:**

The solution is given by a two-soliton interaction formula:

$$
ight]^2}osh\left(rac{\sqrt{c_1}\zeta_1 - \sqrt{c_2}\zeta_2}{2}_2}\zeta_2}{2}
$$


where:
- $\zeta_1 = x - c_1 t - x_1$
- $\zeta_2 = x - c_2 t - x_2$

**Parameters:**
- $c_1 = 6.0$ (velocity of first soliton)
- $c_2 = 2.0$ (velocity of second soliton)
- $x_1 = -2.0$ (initial position of first soliton)
- $x_2 = 2.0$ (initial position of second soliton)

The boundary conditions $g_0, g_1, g_2, g_3$ are derived by evaluating the analytical solution at the appropriate boundaries.

 The implementation uses a Deep MLP architecture with 8 hidden layers of 128 neurons each, tanh activation, and Glorot normal initialization. Inputs (t, x) are normalized to [-1, 1] inside the model's forward method. The optimizer is Optax Adam with a cosine decay learning rate schedule from 1e-3 to 1e-5 over 50,000 steps, chained with global gradient clipping at norm 1.0. The loss function is L_total = λ_PDE(i) * L_PDE + λ_BC * (L_IC + L_BC_left + L_BC_right + L_Neumann), where λ_BC=100.0 is fixed, and λ_PDE follows the curriculum: 0.0 for iterations 0-5000, linearly ramps from 0.0 to 1.0 during 5000-10000, then fixed at 1.0 thereafter. Residual-based Adaptive Refinement (RAR) is implemented every 2500 iterations starting from iteration 10000, evaluating PDE residuals on 10,000 random candidate points and adding the top 200 highest-residual points to the collocation set (initially 2000 LHS points). Training reports relative L2 error every 500 steps on a 256x100 validation grid against the analytical two-soliton solution.
